, we get an incidence correspondence satisfying the conditions of the proposition.

For the converse, we need that
S
is compactly generated. So suppose given a map of sets
f:S\to\mathsf{Fin}_{X}
such that
\Gamma_{f}\subset S\times X
is closed and
\Gamma_{f}\to S
is a local homeomorphism. Take a basic open set
B(K,\mathfrak{U})
, with
\mathfrak{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. We check that
f^{-1}B(K,\mathfrak{U})
is open in
S
. We show first that
the set
N=\{s\in S:\#(f(s)\cap K)\leq n\}
is open in
S
. Since
S
is compactly generated, we may assume
S
itself to be compact Hausdorff. Take
s\in N
. Choose a neighborhood
V
of
f(s)
in
X
, such that each component of
\Gamma_{f}\cap(S\times V)
maps homeomorphically to a neighborhood
W
of
s
. Then
\Gamma_{f}\cap(S\times(K\setminus V))
is compact, and in particular its image in
S
is closed, and does not contain
s
by construction. Removing this closed set from
W
produces a neighborhood of
s
contained in
N
. But then we note that
f^{-1}B(K,\mathfrak{U})
is the intersection of
N
with the
n
open sets given by the projections of
\Gamma_{f}\cap(S\times U_{i})
onto the first factor.
∎

5.1.9 Remark
.

The product of a compactly generated space with a locally compact space is compactly generated, so there is no ambiguity in the proposition regarding what topology to put on
S\times X
.

5.1.10
.

If
A
is clear from context, we may omit it from notation and simply write
\mathsf{Fin}_{X}
for
\mathsf{Fin}_{X;A}
.
In the rest of this section of the paper,
X
will always be a manifold and
A
its boundary, and we consistently omit the boundary from the notation.
Disallowing points on the boundary is natural, since we will consider “gluing” operations on configuration spaces induced by gluing manifolds along their boundary. Such an operation could potentially introduce collisions if points are allowed to lie on the boundary. We want to emphasize in particular one difference in set-up here from the one in
[
McD75
]
(and many other places). McDuff considers spaces of particles on
M
which can be annihilated and created along
\partial M
; we consider particles which can be annihilated and created “at infinity”, whereas
\partial M
simply acts as a “wall”. For example,
\mathsf{Fin}_{[0,1)\times(0,1)}
represents particles moving in the interior of a square, which are allowed to vanish along three of the four sides.

5.2.
Scanning for configuration spaces

5.2.1
.

For any manifold
M
there is a natural
E_{1}
-algebra structure on
\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}
defined by “placing configurations next to each other”. Let us suppose that
M
is connected. If
M
is noncompact, then
\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}
is connected, but when
M
is compact we have

\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}\cong\coprod_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{Conf}_{k}(M^{\circ}\times(0,1)).

In particular, this
E_{1}
-algebra is group-like for noncompact
M
, but not otherwise. The “scanning map”, following terminology introduced by Segal, describes its group-completion.

5.2.2
.

In order to describe the scanning map, it will be convenient to replace
\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}
with a strictly associative “Moore” variant. Fix
\epsilon>0
, and consider the space which parametrizes pairs of
t\in[0,\infty)
, and a configuration
z\in\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,t]}
, such that the configurations are empty above the intervals
[0,\epsilon)
and
(t-\epsilon,t]
. This space is a topological monoid in an evident manner, and we denote this monoid
C
. There is a weak equivalence of
E_{1}
-algebras
C\simeq\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}
, in much the same way as the space of Moore loops is homotopy equivalent to the usual loop space. There is a natural map of topological monoids
C\to\Omega^{\prime}\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
, where
\Omega^{\prime}
denotes the Moore loop space. It takes a configuration
z
parametrized over
[0,t]
to the Moore loop of length
t
in
\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
, whose value at time
t_{0}
is the part of the configuration which lies over the interval
(t_{0}-\epsilon,t_{0}+\epsilon)
. This is the
scanning map
– we are sweeping over a configuration using a scanner with field of vision of size
\epsilon
.

5.2.3 Theorem

(Segal, McDuff)
.

The scanning map
C\to\Omega^{\prime}\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
is a group-completion. That is, the group-completion of the
E_{1}
-algebra
\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}
is weakly equivalent to
\Omega\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times\mathbf{R}}
.

5.2.4
.

Before giving the proof of
5.2.3
we want to recall explicitly the bar construction. If
M
is a well-pointed topological monoid, we define the
bar construction

BM
as a quotient

\Big(\coprod_{p\geq 0}\Delta^{p}\times M^{p}\Big)\!\Big/\!\sim

where
\sim
denotes the equivalence relation generated by the following identifications:

•

((0,w_{1},\ldots,w_{p}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\sim((w_{1},\ldots,w_{p}),(m_{2},\ldots,m_{p}))
,

•

((w_{0},\ldots,w_{i-1},0,w_{i+1},\ldots,w_{p}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\sim

hello

((w_{0},\ldots,w_{i-1},w_{i+1},\ldots,w_{p}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{i}\cdot m_{i+1},\ldots,m_{p}))
,

•

((w_{0},\ldots,w_{p-1},0),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p}))\sim((w_{0},\ldots,w_{p-1}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{p-1}))
,

•

((w_{0},\ldots,w_{p}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{i-1},e,m_{i+1},\ldots,m_{p}))\sim

hello

(((w_{0},\ldots,w_{i-1}+w_{i},\ldots,w_{p}),(m_{1},\ldots,m_{i-1},m_{i+1},\ldots,m_{p})).

Here the
(w_{i})
are coordinates on the
p
-simplex:
0\leq w_{i}\leq 1
,
\sum w_{i}=1
. The space
BM
is, in an evident way, the geometric realization of a simplicial space. The bar construction is often instead defined as the geometric realization of the corresponding semisimplicial space (i.e. omitting the fourth identification in the definition of
\sim
). Then the construction given here would be called the reduced bar construction. For a well-pointed topological monoid the reduced and unreduced bar constructions are weakly equivalent
[
Seg73
, Appendix 2]
.

5.2.5
.

For any connected based space,
X
there is a natural weak equivalence
B\Omega^{\prime}X\to X
, which is defined as follows. Given a point
(w_{0},\ldots,w_{p})\in\Delta^{p}
and a
p
-tuple of Moore loops in
X

\gamma_{1}:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to X,\qquad\gamma_{2}:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to X,\qquad\ldots\qquad\gamma_{p}:[t_{p-1},t_{p}]\to X,

where
t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{p}
, first concatenate these loops to a loop
\gamma:[t_{0},t_{p}]\to X
. The image of this pair in
X
is then the evaluation
\gamma(\sum w_{i}t_{i})\in X
.

Proof of Theorem
5.2.3
.

We prove that the scanning map
C\to\Omega^{\prime}\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
of §
5.2.2
is a group-completion. Equivalently, the composition

BC\to B\Omega^{\prime}\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}\to\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}

is a weak equivalence.
Let us denote this composition
\sigma
.

Consider first a based map
f:S^{q}\to\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
. We want to find a lift
g:S^{q}\to BC
such that
f
is homotopic to
\sigma\circ g
.

Choose (using compactness) a finite open cover
S^{q}=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}U_{\lambda}
, such that there is a positive real
r>0
and for each
\lambda
an element
t_{\lambda}\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with the following property:

for any
x\in U_{\lambda}
, the part of the configuration
f(x)
above time-coordinate
(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
is empty.
Choose also a partition of unity
w_{\lambda}
subordinate to the cover.

Consider
x\in U_{\lambda_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{\lambda_{k}}
(and assume it lies in no other
U_{\lambda}
). We assume that

t_{\lambda_{0}}<\ldots<t_{\lambda_{k}}.

By taking the configurations above the intervals
[t_{\lambda_{0}},t_{\lambda_{1}}]
, …,
[t_{\lambda_{k-1}},t_{\lambda_{k}}]
, respectively, and stretching them all by a factor
\tfrac{\epsilon}{r}
, we get a
k
-tuple of elements of our monoid
C
. We define
g(x)\in BC
to be the element defined by this
k
-tuple of elements of
C
, together with
(w_{\lambda_{0}}(x),w_{\lambda_{1}}(x),\ldots,w_{\lambda_{k}}(x))\in\Delta^{k}
.

One checks that this is compatible with the identifications in the bar construction, and glues together well along overlaps for our open cover. In order that
g
is a based map, we may insist that the base-point of
S^{q}
lies only in one of the sets
U_{\lambda}
(if this is not the case, just delete the base point from all but one of the open sets in the cover).

The configuration given by
\sigma(g(x))
is a “zoomed in” version of
f(x)
. More precisely,
\sigma(g(x))
is the part of the configuration
f(x)
which lies over a small interval of radius
r
, centered at the point
\sum w_{\lambda}(x)t_{\lambda}
. In particular,
\sigma\circ g
is evidently homotopic to
f
.

For injectivity on homotopy groups, consider instead a map
g:S^{q}\to BC
and a nullhomotopy
f:D^{q+1}\to\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
of
\sigma\circ g
. The same construction as above allows us to lift
f
up to homotopy to a map
f^{\prime}:D^{q+1}\to BC
. We should construct a homotopy between
g
and
f^{\prime}|_{S^{q}}
. Consider an element
x\in S^{q}
, and let us suppose that
x\in U_{\lambda_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{\lambda_{k}}
(and
x
lies in no other
U_{\lambda}
). Now
g(x)
consists of a
p
-tuple of elements of
C
over intervals
[t_{0},t_{1}]
,…,
[t_{p-1},t_{p}]
, and a weight
w\in\Delta^{p}
. First, we continuously stretch all of these intervals by a factor
\tfrac{\epsilon}{r}
. After stretching, they can be nontrivially written as a product in
C
, with the time-parameters
t_{\lambda_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, providing the breakpoints where we can split up the intervals. This gives us instead an equivalent point of the bar construction, given by
(p+k)
-tuple of elements of
C
and a weight vector in
\Delta^{p+k}
, with
k
zeroes inserted in the weight vector. Then continuously change the weights from the given weight vector
w
to the one specified by the partition of unity
w_{\lambda}
, lowering the “old” weights to zero and raising the “new” weights from zero to their correct value
w_{\lambda_{i}}(x)
,
i=1,\ldots,k
. ∎

5.2.6 Remark
.

In the final paragraph of the preceding proof, it is important that we work with the reduced bar construction as opposed to the unreduced bar construction. In the construction we have continuously varying time-parameters
t_{0},\ldots,t_{p}
and
t_{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,t_{\lambda_{k}}
, and nothing in the construction prevents an overlap
t_{i}=t_{\lambda_{j}}
for some
i,j
, which allows two such time-coordinates to “swap places”. This has the effect of changing the slot in which we insert a zero coordinate on the simplex, and in the unreduced bar construction this would introduce a discontinuity in the final homotopy where the “old” weights are lowered and the “new” weights are increased. This is exactly solved by passing to the reduced bar construction, since an overlap
t_{i}=t_{\lambda_{j}}
corresponds to one of the factors being the base-point of the monoid
C
.

5.2.7 Proposition
.

Let
B^{n}
be the open unit ball in
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. Then
\mathsf{Fin}_{B^{n}}\simeq S^{n}
.

Proof.

First, define a continuous function
\phi:\mathsf{Fin}_{B^{n}}\to(0,1]
as follows: if
z\subset B^{n}
is a finite subset with at least two elements, then
\phi(z)
is the
second
smallest distance to the origin among the points in the configuration. (Note that this number is necessarily strictly positive, unlike the smallest distance.) If
z
has cardinality
\leq 1
, then
\phi(z)=1
. In formulas,
\phi(z)=\sup\{r\in[0,1]:|z\cap(rB^{n})|\leq 1\}
.

Then consider the continuous function
F:\mathsf{Fin}_{B^{n}}\to\mathsf{Fin}_{B^{n}}
which rescales a configuration by a factor
1/\phi(z)
, and in particular forgets about any points which lie outside the unit ball after rescaling. One verifies that the function
F
defines a deformation retraction down to the subspace of
\mathsf{Fin}_{B^{n}}
consisting of configurations of cardinality
\leq 1
.

But the subspace of
\mathsf{Fin}_{B^{n}}
consisting of configurations of cardinality
\leq 1
is in fact homeomorphic to an
n
-sphere. Indeed, the subspace of configurations of cardinality
1
is just
B^{n}
itself, and adding in the empty configuration amounts to forming the one-point compactification. ∎

5.2.8 Corollary
.

The group-completion of the
E_{n}
-algebra
\mathsf{Fin}_{[0,1]^{n}}=\coprod_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{Conf}_{k}((0,1)^{n})
is
\Omega^{n}S^{n}
.

Proof.

Apply
5.2.3
iteratively
n
times to see that
\mathsf{Fin}_{[0,1]^{n}}
group-completes to
\Omega^{n}\mathsf{Fin}_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}
.
5.2.7
identifies this with
\Omega^{n}S^{n}
.
∎

5.3.
Hurwitz spaces

5.3.1
.

We now want to generalize the results of the preceding subsection from configuration spaces to
Hurwitz spaces
. Although we only need these results for branched double covers, we will consider more generally an arbitrary finite group
G
and a space of branched
G
-covers
E\to M
. Moreover, as explained in
Section

4.4
, we should work with spaces parametrizing in addition the datum of a
G
-equivariant map
E\to X
, for some
G
-space
X
.

5.3.2
.

Let us for the rest of this section fix the following data: a finite group
G
, a conjugation-invariant subset
c\subseteq G
specifying “allowed monodromies” which we assume to generate
G
, and a based
G
-space
X
, which we assume to be connected. Since
G
,
c
and
X
will be fixed throughout the arguments, we consistently omit them from notation, writing
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
where something like
\mathsf{Hur}_{G}^{c}(M,X)
would be more logical.

5.3.3
.

Let
M
be an oriented 2-manifold, possibly with boundary. Let us first informally describe the space
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
of interest to us. A point of
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
will consists of a finite subset
z
of
M^{\circ}
, a branched
G
-cover
E\to M
with branch locus
z
such that all local monodromies are in the set
c
, and a
G
-equivariant map
E\to X
. Moreover, since we will want to glue surfaces along their boundaries, we insist also that the
G
-torsor
\partial E\to\partial M
comes with a chosen trivialization, and the map
\partial E\to X
is constant at the basepoint. As in the case of
\mathsf{Fin}_{M}
we want to topologize this in such a way that branch points can vanish at infinity. To make this precise, we describe
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
as a topological stack.

5.3.4 Definition
.

Define
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
to be the functor
k\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{Grpd}
assigning to a compactly generated space
S
the following groupoid: an object consists of a closed subset
\Gamma\subset S\times M
such that
\Gamma\cap(S\times\partial M)=\varnothing
and
\Gamma\to S
is a local homeomorphism with finite fibers, a branched
G
-cover
E\to S\times M
with branch locus
\Gamma
and all local monodromies in the set
c
, a trivialization
\partial E\cong G\times S\times\partial M
of the
G
-torsor
\partial E\to S\times\partial M
, and a
G
-equivariant map
E\to X
taking
\partial E
to the basepoint of
X
. Morphisms in the groupoid are isomorphisms of this data.

5.3.5 Proposition
.

The functor
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
is a paratopological stack in the sense of Noohi.

Proof.

There is an evident forgetful map
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}\to\mathsf{Fin}_{M}
, and
\mathsf{Fin}_{M}
is certainly a topological stack, being represented by an actual topological space. To show that
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
is a paratopological stack, we “build” it from
\mathsf{Fin}_{M}
in simple steps: start with the universal incidence correspondence
\Gamma\subset\mathsf{Fin}_{M}\times M
, consider the mapping stack
\mathrm{map}(\mathsf{Fin}_{M}\times M\setminus\Gamma,BG)
(i.e. the stack of unbranched
G
-covers of
\mathsf{Fin}_{M}\times M\setminus\Gamma
), take the open and closed substack where local monodromies are in
c
, observe that the universal
G
-torsor extends uniquely to a branched cover of
\mathsf{Fin}_{M}\times M
, form the mapping stack from the universal branched
G
-cover to
X
, and take
G
-fixed points.
∎

5.3.6 Remark
.

Presumably
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
is actually a topological stack, but we will not need this, and the argument just given only proves existence as a paratopological stack: Noohi only proves existence of mapping stacks in this generality
[
Noo10
]
.

5.3.7 Remark
.

We will consistently describe stacks as sheaves of groupoids rather than (more correctly) as categories fibered in groupoids.

5.3.8
.

Describing
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
as a stack is not just a convenient way of specifying the topology on
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
. In the case that
\partial M=\varnothing
, the functor
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
is simply not represented by a topological space, due to the presence of objects with nontrivial automorphisms, and we are obligated to treat it as a stack. When
\partial M\neq\varnothing
, the datum of a trivialization of
E\to M
along
\partial M
rigidifies the moduli problem and makes
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
representable by a space.

5.3.9
.

Consider a
1
-manifold
L
. Then
\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}
is an
E_{1}
-algebra in the same way as
\mathsf{Fin}_{M\times[0,1]}
. Again, we replace it with a strictly associative version. Fix
\epsilon>0
and let
H
be the topological monoid parametrizing the data of
t\in[0,\infty)
, and a point of
\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,t]}
, which we demand to be standard over the intervals
[0,\epsilon)
and
(t-\epsilon,t]
. As before, there is a scanning map
H\to\Omega^{\prime}\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
.

5.3.10 Theorem
.

Suppose that
L
is an interval (closed, open or half-open). The scanning map
H\to\Omega^{\prime}\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
is the group-completion of
H
. That is, the group-completion of the
E_{1}
-algebra
\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}
is weakly equivalent to
\Omega\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times\mathbf{R}}
.

Proof in the case that
L
is an open interval.

As in the proof of
5.2.3
, we want to show that
\sigma:BH\to\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
is a weak equivalence. So take a based map
f:S^{q}\to\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
, giving a branched
G
-cover of
S^{q}\times L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, branched over a closed subset
\Gamma_{f}
. Choose as before a finite open cover
S^{q}=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}U_{\lambda}
, such that there is a positive real
r>0
and for each
\lambda
an element
t_{\lambda}\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
, with the following property:
for each
\lambda
,

\Gamma_{f}\cap(U_{\lambda}\times L\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r))=\varnothing.

Without loss of generality, each
U_{\lambda}
is contractible. Then for each
\lambda
we get a
trivial

G
-torsor over
U_{\lambda}\times L\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
, since we have an unbranched cover of a contractible space. We choose a trivialization for each
\lambda
.

Now we need to care for the map to the target space
X
. For each
\lambda
we get an equivariant map

G\times U_{\lambda}\times L\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)\to X,

and since
X
is connected it is necessarily equivariantly nullhomotopic. Plugging in this nullhomotopy in the middle of the interval
(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
we may arrange that the map to
X
is constant over the smaller interval of radius
r/2
, say. To get this nullhomotopy to be defined on all of
S^{q}
, we apply the shrinking lemma: there exists an open refinement by subsets
U_{\lambda}^{\prime}\subset U_{\lambda}
, and for each
\lambda
a bump function on
S^{q}
which is
1
on
U_{\lambda}^{\prime}
and with support in
U_{\lambda}
. Taking a nullhomotopy “mollified” by this bump function, we finally arrange that the map to the target space is constant above
U_{\lambda}^{\prime}\times L\times(t_{\lambda}-\tfrac{r}{2},t_{\lambda}+\tfrac{r}{2})
. We then take
r/2
to be our new
r
, and
U_{\lambda}^{\prime}
to be our new
U_{\lambda}
. These homotopies for different
\lambda
may be done independently of each other, since without loss of generality the family of intervals
(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
are disjoint.

After all this finagling, we are in a situation exactly like the one for ordinary configurations, and by exactly the same arguments as in the proof of
5.2.3
the map
f:S^{q}\to\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
lifts up to homotopy to a map
g:S^{q}\to BH
.

Suppose instead that we have a map
g:S^{q}\to BH
and a nullhomotopy
f:D^{q+1}\to\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
of
\sigma\circ g
. We carry out the same procedure as just described to homotope
f
to a map
f_{(1)}
for which there is an open cover
U_{\lambda}
of
D^{q+1}
, such that we can choose a trivialization of the
G
-torsor over
U_{\lambda}\times L\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
for all
\lambda
, and the map to the target space is constant over this region. The construction “lifts” in an evident way to a homotopy between
g
and a new map
g_{(1)}
, in such a way that
f_{(1)}
is instead a nullhomotopy of
\sigma\circ g_{(1)}
. Then by the same construction as earlier,
f_{(1)}
lifts up to homotopy to a map
D^{q+1}\to BH
, and the construction in the final paragraph of the proof of
5.2.3
shows that this lifting can be homotoped to a nullhomotopy of
g_{(1)}
.
∎

5.3.11
.

The argument just given works equally well when
L
is a half-open interval, but it is also less interesting as
\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}
is contractible in this case. However, it needs to be modified in the case that
L=[a,b]
is a
closed
interval. The problem arises in the first paragraph of the proof, when arguing that we can choose a trivialization of the
G
-torsor over
U_{\lambda}\times L\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
for each
\lambda
. When
L
has boundary, this
G
-torsor already comes with two trivializations; one over
U_{\lambda}\times\{a\}\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
and one over
U_{\lambda}\times\{b\}\times(t_{\lambda}-r,t_{\lambda}+r)
. In general, there is no reason for these two trivializations to be compatible with each other, which they would need to be for the rest of the argument to carry through. Let us now explain how to tweak the set-up to treat the case that
L
is closed.

5.3.12 Definition
.

Let
M
be an oriented surface,
J\subset\partial M
an embedded interval which is closed in
M
. We write
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}^{J}
for the stack assigning to a compactly generated space
S
the groupoid of branched
G
-covers
p:E\to S\times M
just as in
5.3.4
, except we only insist on a trivialization

p^{-1}(S\times(\partial M\setminus J^{\circ}))\cong G\times S\times(\partial M\setminus J^{\circ}),

and the equivariant map
E\to X
is only required to take
p^{-1}(S\times(\partial M\setminus J^{\circ}))
to the basepoint of
X
.

5.3.13
.

There is an evident restriction map
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}^{J}\to\mathrm{map}_{\ast}(J/\partial J,[X/G])
, where
[X/G]
denotes the quotient stack of
G
acting on
X
. Indeed, an
S
-point of the right-hand side consists of a
G
-torsor over
S\times J
with a trivialization over
S\times\partial J
, and a
G
-equivariant map from the total space to
X
taking the inverse image of
S\times\partial J
to the basepoint of
X
.

5.3.14 Lemma
.

The restriction map
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}^{J}\to\mathrm{map}_{\ast}(J/\partial J,[X/G])
is a fibration.

Proof.

Let
S
be a compactly generated space, and consider a lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hur}_{M}^{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\times S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{map}_{\ast}(J/\partial J,[X/G])}

The map
S\to\mathsf{Hur}_{M}^{J}
furnishes in particular a branched
G
-cover
p:E\to S\times M
; then
I\times E
is naturally a branched
G
-cover of
I\times S\times M
, which is correctly trivialized to yield the first piece of data to define a lifting in the diagram. To fill in the diagram, we need moreover a
G
-equivariant map
I\times E\to X
which agrees with the specified values on
\{0\}\times E
and
I\times p^{-1}(S\times J)
; that is, we should argue that the inclusion
p^{-1}(S\times{J})\hookrightarrow E
is a
G
-cofibration (satisfies the equivariant homotopy extension property). To see this, one observes that
p^{-1}(S\times J)
admits a neighborhood on which
G
acts freely, and taking the quotient by
G
on this neighborhood gives back an open neighborhood of
S\times J
in
S\times M
. But
S\times J\hookrightarrow S\times M
is an ordinary cofibration.∎

Proof of
5.3.10
in case
L={[a,b]}
is a closed interval.

A consequence of
5.3.14
is that there is a diagram of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}^{\{a\}\times[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[X/G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{\{a\}\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega[X/G].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Indeed, the top row is obtained directly from
5.3.14
with
M=L\times[0,1]
and
J=\{a\}\times[0,1]
. The bottom row is instead obtained by once looping the fiber sequence given by
5.3.14
for
M=L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
,
J=\{a\}\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. Note in particular that
\partial J=\varnothing
so
\mathrm{map}_{\ast}(J/\partial J,[X/G])\simeq[X/G]
in this case. The three vertical maps are given by the respective scanning maps. Now the entire diagram deloops to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}^{\{a\}\times[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X/G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{\{a\}\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[X/G]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

By an argument exactly like the one in the proof given immediately after
5.3.10
, the middle vertical map is an equivalence. Indeed, the issue explained in §
5.3.11
is no longer a problem, precisely because in the space
\mathsf{Hur}_{L\times[0,1]}^{\{a\}\times[0,1]}
we no longer insist on a trivialization along
\{a\}\times[0,1]
. Thus, the leftmost vertical map is a weak equivalence, too.
∎

5.4.
Local Hurwitz space

5.4.1
.

Just as in
5.2.7
, let us now analyze the topology of the Hurwitz space
\mathsf{Hur}_{M}
when
M=B^{2}
is an open disk. We will explicitly determine the homotopy type of the paratopological stack
\mathsf{Hur}_{B^{2}}
. All the reader needs to know about the homotopy theory of stacks is that one can consistently assign a homotopy type to a paratopological stack
[
Noo12
]
, and that the homotopy type of a quotient stack
[Y/\Gamma]
is the homotopy quotient
Y/\!/\Gamma
.

5.4.2 Construction
.

Introduce the notation

X(c):=\coprod_{g\in c}X^{g}

for the disjoint union of the sets of
g
-fixed points, for
g\in c
. We consider
X(c)
itself as a
G
-space in the natural way: multiplication by an element
h
maps
X^{g}
to
X^{hgh^{-1}}
.

5.4.3 Construction
.

A point of
\mathrm{map}(S^{1},X/\!/G)
can be identified with a
G
-torsor
E\to S^{1}
and a
G
-equivariant map
E\to X
. We write
\mathrm{map}(S^{1},X/\!/G)_{0}
for the subspace on which the corresponding map
E\to X
is nullhomotopic on each connected component. Note that there is a weak equivalence

\mathrm{map}(S^{1},X/\!/G)_{0}\cong\big(\coprod_{g\in G}X^{g}\big)/\!/G=X(G)/\!/G.

For any conjugation-invariant
c\subseteq G
we therefore get a natural “evaluation” map

S^{1}\times(X(c)/\!/G)\hookrightarrow S^{1}\times(X(G)/\!/G)\simeq S^{1}\times\mathrm{map}(S^{1},X/\!/G)_{0}\to X/\!/G.

5.4.4 Theorem
.

There is a homotopy pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}\times X(c)/\!/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/\!/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(c)/\!/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Hur}_{B^{2}}}

in which the top horizontal arrow is the evaluation map of
5.4.3
, and the left vertical arrow is the projection to the second factor.

Proof.

The Hurwitz space deformation retracts down to the substack on which there is at most one branch point, by the same argument as in
5.2.7
. To analyze this substack, we cover it by two open pieces.

Firstly, there is the locus where there is no branch point at the origin. We claim that this substack is homotopy equivalent to
X/\!/G
. Indeed, by an argument much like that of
5.2.7
, but using the
smallest
distance to the origin instead of the second-smallest distance, this space deformation retracts down to the subspace where there are no branch points at all. Thus, we get a trivial
G
-torsor over a disk and an equivariant map from the total space to
X
, which is the same as a map from a disk to
[X/G]
.

Secondly, there is the open locus where there is exactly one branch point on the disk. We claim that this substack is homotopy equivalent to
X(c)/\!/G
. We may for this calculation replace the open disk with a closed disk, and insist that there is a unique branch point on the interior. If we add the auxilliary datum of a trivialization of the
G
-torsor over some fixed point on the boundary of the disk, then we get a space of the homotopy type of
X(c)
. Indeed, note first that after such a choice, the monodromy around the branch point becomes a well-defined element
g\in c
. Moreover, the branched cover is a finite union of disks, and an equivariant map to
X
from this union is determined up to homotopy by where it sends the center of one of the disks: this will be an element of
X^{g}
, yielding the space
X(c)=\coprod_{g\in c}X^{g}
. But then forgetting the auxiliary datum of a trivialization over a point on the boundary amounts to taking the stack quotient by
G
.

These open subsets are glued along their intersection, whose homotopy type is
S^{1}\times X(c)/\!/G
, by an argument much like the previous paragraph. ∎

5.4.5
.

We remind the reader of the “clutching construction”, which says that rank
d
vector bundles on a sphere
S^{n}
are classified by maps
S^{n-1}\to\mathrm{GL}(d)
. The same construction works not just for vector bundles but for fibrations: for any space
F
, fibrations over
S^{n}
with fiber
F
are in bijection with maps
S^{n-1}\to\mathrm{hAut}(F)
, up to homotopy. Explicitly, a map
S^{n-1}\to\mathrm{hAut}(F)
gives an automorphism of the fibration
S^{n-1}\times F\to S^{n-1}
, and the fibration over
S^{n}
associated to such a clutching function can be described as the homotopy pushout

F\coprod^{h}_{S^{n-1}\times F}F,

where one of the maps is the projection to the second factor, and the other is the projection to the second factor precomposed with the chosen automorphism of
S^{n-1}\times F
.

Proof of
5.0.3
.

Let
X=KA
,
G=\Sigma_{2}
, and let
c
consist only of the non-identity element. Then there is a weak equivalence of
E_{2}
-algebras

\mathsf{Hur}_{[0,1]^{2}}\simeq\coprod_{g\geq 0}H_{g}^{0,1}(KA).

By two applications of
5.3.10
, the
2
-fold delooping of this
E_{2}
-algebra is
\mathsf{Hur}_{B^{2}}
. By
5.4.4
, the double delooping therefore has the homotopy type of the pushout

KA/\!/\langle\iota\rangle\coprod^{h}_{S^{1}\times B\Sigma_{2}}B\Sigma_{2},

using that
(KA)^{\Sigma_{2}}=\ast
. The map
S^{1}\times B\Sigma_{2}\to KA/\!/\langle\iota\rangle
factors through the map
B\Sigma_{2}\to KA/\!/\langle\iota\rangle
induced by the inclusion of the
\Sigma_{2}
-fixed points in
KA
, so we may rewrite the pushout as

KA/\!/\langle\iota\rangle\coprod^{h}_{B\Sigma_{2}}B\Sigma_{2}\coprod^{h}_{S^{1}\times B\Sigma_{2}}B\Sigma_{2}

and to finish the proof of
5.0.3
we should only identify the pushout
B\Sigma_{2}\coprod^{h}_{S^{1}\times B\Sigma_{2}}B\Sigma_{2}
with the space
W
defined in
5.0.2
. But the latter pushout is an instance of the clutching construction described in §
5.4.5
, in the case
n=2
and
F=B\Sigma_{2}
. Indeed, one leg of the pushout is projection to the second factor, and the other leg is the “evaluation” map of
5.4.3
. It is not hard to check that the evaluation can be identified with the precomposition of projection to the second factor with the unique nontrivial automorphism of the fibration
S^{1}\times B\Sigma_{2}\to S^{1}
, so that the pushout may be identified with the unique nontrivial fibration over
S^{2}
with fiber
B\Sigma_{2}
— but this is exactly the space
W
defined in
5.0.2
.
∎

5.4.6 Remark
.

More generally, the pushout of
5.4.4
can always be rewritten in the form

\mathsf{Hur}_{B^{2}}\simeq X/\!/G\coprod^{h}_{X(c)/\!/G}W(c),

where
W(c)
is the total space of a fibration over
S^{2}
with fiber
X(c)/\!/G
. This fibration is described by the clutching function
S^{1}\to\mathrm{hAut}(X(c)/\!/G)
given by identifying
X(c)/\!/G
with a union of connected components of
\mathrm{map}(S^{1},X/\!/G)
, on which
S^{1}
acts by rotation.

6.
Double loop space homology

6.0.1
.

In the preceding section of the paper, we showed that the group-completion of the monoid
\coprod_{g\geq 0}H_{g}^{0,1}(KA)
is the double loop space
\Omega^{2}\,W(A)
, if
A
is a connected chain complex without
2
-torsion. In particular, the group-completion theorem identifies the stable homology of
H_{g}^{0,1}(KA)
with the homology of the space
\Omega^{2}_{0}\,W(A)
. The goal of this section is to calculate the rational homology
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}_{0}\,W(A),\mathbf{Q})
as an explicit analytic functor of
A
. This result will appear as
6.4.1
.

6.0.2
.

The proof of
6.4.1
is an application of Koszul duality and rational homotopy theory. To fix conventions and for the reader’s convenience, we begin by briefly recalling the notions of quadratic algebras and coalgebras, and Koszul duality of such algebras. We work over
\mathbf{Q}
, although all results here are valid more generally over any base field. The algebraic results recalled here are all quite standard. The original reference is the work of Priddy
[
Pri70
]
; for a textbook account, see Loday–Vallette
[
LV12
, Chapter 3]
. We then discuss the notion of a
Koszul space
, introduced by Berglund
[
Ber14
]
, and prove that
W(A)
is an example of a Koszul space. After all this preparation, we state and prove
6.4.1
.

6.1.
Quadratic algebras

6.1.1
.

Let
V
be a graded vector space, and
R\subseteq V\otimes V
a subspace. We denote by
A(V,R)
the associative algebra
\mathsf{T}V/\langle R\rangle
, where
\mathsf{T}V
denotes the tensor algebra on
V
and
\langle R\rangle
is the two-sided ideal generated by
R\subseteq V^{\otimes 2}\subset\mathsf{T}V
. An associative algebra of this form will be called a
quadratic algebra
. Being quadratic is a property of an associative algebra, not extra structure, as such a quadratic presentation is unique if it exists;
V
can be recovered as the space of indecomposables in the algebra.

6.1.2
.

A quadratic algebra
A(V,R)
has two natural gradings: one arising from the grading of
V
, which we refer to as the
homological degree
, and one given by putting
V^{\otimes n}
in degree
n
, which we refer to as the
length grading
, or the
tensor length
. If
A
is a quadratic algebra, then we denote by
A_{i}(n)\subset A
the subspace of homological degree
i
and tensor length
n
. We say that
A
is of
finite type
if
\dim A_{i}(n)<\infty
for all
i
and
n
.
The
Hilbert–Poincaré series
of a quadratic algebra
A
of finite type is defined by

A(t,z)=\sum\dim(A_{i}(n))t^{n}(-z)^{i}.

The fact that
(-z)^{i}
appears in the formula rather than
z^{i}
is due to the fact that the Koszul sign rule is enforced with respect to the homological grading, cf. the discussion in §
2.4.8
. Similarly,
t^{n}
appears rather than
(-t)^{n}
, since the Koszul sign rule is not enforced with respect to the length grading.

6.1.3
.

To the same data
(V,R)
we also associate a
quadratic coalgebra

C(V,R)
. Explictly,
C(V,R)
is the subcoalgebra of the tensor coalgebra
\mathsf{T}^{c}V
which in tensor length
n
is given by the intersection
\cap_{i+2+j=n}V^{\otimes i}\otimes R\otimes V^{\otimes j}
. Again, being quadratic is a property of a coalgebra, not additional structure. Quadratic coalgebras are similarly bigraded, and an identical formula defines the Hilbert–Poincaré series of a quadratic coalgebra of finite type.

6.1.4
.

If
A=A(V,R)
is a quadratic algebra, then we define its
quadratic dual
coalgebra to be the quadratic coalgebra
A^{\text{!`}}=C(V[-1],R[-2])
. Similarly, the quadratic dual algebra of
C=C(V,R)
is
C^{\text{!`}}=A(V[1],R[2])
.

6.2.
Bar-cobar duality and Koszul duality

6.2.1
.

We denote by
B
and
\Omega
the bar and cobar functors
[
LV12
, Section 2.2]
. The bar construction
B
is a functor from augmented dg algebras to coaugmented conilpotent dg coalgebras, and the cobar functor is its left adjoint. The important fact about the bar and cobar constructions is that they are very close to being inverses of each other up to quasi-isomorphism. Recall that a coaugmented coalgebra
C
is
n
-connected if the coaugmentation coideal
\overline{C}
vanishes in degree
\leq n
.

6.2.2 Theorem
.

The bar and cobar functors preserve quasi-isomorphisms in the following sense:

(1)

If
A\to A^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism of dg algebras, then
BA\to BA^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(2)

If
C\to C^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism of
1
-connected dg coalgebras, then
\Omega C\to\Omega C^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(3)

The counit map
\Omega BA\to A
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(4)

The unit map
C\to B\Omega C
is a quasi-isomorphism.

6.2.3
.

The bar and cobar constructions predate the notion of Koszul duality. The original motivation for introducing the cobar construction was in order to formulate the following theorem
[
Ada56
]
:

6.2.4 Theorem

(Adams)
.

Let
X
be a
1
-connected CW complex. Then
\Omega C_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Z})\simeq C_{\bullet}(\Omega^{\prime}X,\mathbf{Z})
, where
C_{\bullet}(\Omega^{\prime}X,\mathbf{Z})
is considered as a dg algebra using the concatenation of Moore loops.

6.2.5 Remark
.

The conclusion of the theorem holds also for
X
not necessarily simply connected
[
RZ18
]
.

6.2.6 Definition
.

For any quadratic algebra
A
there is a natural morphism
A^{\text{!`}}\to BA
, and for a quadratic coalgebra
C
there is a natural morphism
\Omega C\to C^{\text{!`}}
. A quadratic algebra
A
is said to be
Koszul
if
A^{\text{!`}}\to BA
is a quasi-isomorphism. Similarly, a quadratic coalgebra
C
is Koszul if
\Omega C\to C^{\text{!`}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. A quadratic algebra
A
is Koszul if and only if
A^{\text{!`}}
is Koszul; we say that
A
and
A^{\text{!`}}
are
Koszul dual
.

6.2.7
.

Suppose we are given a coaugmented coalgebra
C
, an augmented algebra
A
, and a homomorphism of algebras
\tau\colon\Omega C\to A
(equivalently, a morphism of coalgebras
C\to BA
), i.e. what is called a
twisting morphism
from
C
to
A
. To this data, one can associate naturally a differential on the tensor product
C\otimes A
. We write
C\otimes_{\tau}A
for the tensor product equipped with this twisting differential. If
\tau
is a quasi-isomorphism, then
C\otimes_{\tau}A\simeq\mathbf{Q}
. In particular, applying this to the canonical quasi-isomorphism
\tau:\Omega C\to C^{\text{!`}}
for a Koszul coalgebra
C
, taking alternating sums of dimensions in
C\otimes_{\tau}C^{\text{!`}}
gives a relation between the Hilbert–Poincaré series of
C
and
C^{\text{!`}}
, which reads as follows:

6.2.8 Theorem
.

Let
C
be a Koszul coalgebra of finite type. Then

C(t,z)C^{\text{\emph{\text{!`}}}}(\tfrac{t}{z},z)=1.

6.2.9 Remark
.

This result is most often stated without the additional homological grading, which corresponds to setting
z=-1
, yielding
C(t)C^{\text{!`}}(-t)=1
.

6.2.10
.

It will be important for us that the construction of §
6.2.7
is
natural
, and that therefore so is
6.2.8
. For example, if a group
G
acts on the coalgebra
C
and the algebra
A
, and the morphism
\tau\colon\Omega C\to A
is
G
-equivariant, then
C\otimes_{\tau}A
is a chain complex of
G
-modules. Thus, we obtain a refined form of
6.2.8
in which the coefficients of
C(t,z)
and
C^{\text{!`}}(\tfrac{t}{z},z)
are taken in
R(G)
, the Grothendieck ring of representations of
G
. Even more abstractly, we will be interested in quadratic (co)algebras
A(V,R)
(
C(V,R)
) in which
V
and
R
are both
analytic functors
. We may think of this either as having a quadratic algebra in the category of analytic functors, or as having an analytic functor taking values in the category of quadratic algebras. Either way, we obtain a refined form of
6.2.8
in which the coefficients of
C(t,z)
and
C^{\text{!`}}(\tfrac{t}{z},z)
are taken in
\widehat{\Lambda}
, the Grothendieck ring of analytic functors.

6.3.
Koszul spaces in general, and the space
W(A)
in particular

6.3.1 Definition
.

A simply connected based space
X
of finite
\mathbf{Q}
-type is said to be
Koszul
if
X
is formal in the sense of rational homotopy theory, and
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
is a Koszul algebra.

6.3.2 Example
.

The following are simple examples of Koszul spaces:

(1)

Spheres,

(2)

Eilenberg–MacLane spaces,

(3)

Wedges of Koszul spaces,

(4)

Products of Koszul spaces.

6.3.3
.

All we will need about Koszul spaces is the following simple proposition; see Berglund
[
Ber14
, Theorem 4]
for a more general statement.

6.3.4 Proposition
.

Let
X
be a Koszul space. Then
H_{\bullet}(\Omega X,\mathbf{Q})
, with the algebra structure given by concatenation of loops, is the quadratic dual of the Koszul coalgebra
H_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
.

Proof.

By Adams’
6.2.4
we have
C_{\bullet}(\Omega X,\mathbf{Z})\simeq\Omega C_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Z})
. Now
X
is formal over
\mathbf{Q}
, so
C^{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})\simeq H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
, and for
X
of finite
\mathbf{Q}
-type we may dualize this to an equivalence of coalgebras
C_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})\simeq H_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
. Hence,
C_{\bullet}(\Omega X,\mathbf{Q})\simeq\Omega C_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})\simeq\Omega H_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
using
6.2.2
and that
X
is simply connected. Since
H_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
is in addition a Koszul coalgebra,
\Omega H_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
is equivalent to the quadratic dual of
H_{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
.
∎

6.3.5
.

We will now prove that the space
W(A)
is Koszul.

6.3.6 Lemma
.

Let
A
be a
1
-connected chain complex. The space
W(A)
is simply connected.

Proof.

Apply the Seifert–van Kampen theorem to the covering of
W(A)
by
W
(simply connected) and
KA/\!/\langle\iota\rangle
(fundamental group
\Sigma_{2}
), with intersection
B\Sigma_{2}
.
∎

6.3.7 Lemma
.

Let
A
be a
1
-connected chain complex. After localizing away from the prime
2
, there is a homotopy equivalence
W(A)\simeq S^{2}\vee KA/\langle\iota\rangle
.

Proof.

There is a map
W(A)\to S^{2}\vee KA/\langle\iota\rangle
defined by the projections
W\to S^{2}
,
B\Sigma_{2}\to\ast
, and
KA/\!/\langle\iota\rangle\to KA/\langle\iota\rangle
. Each of these maps is an isomorphism on homology with
\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
-coefficients, and therefore so is
W(A)\to S^{2}\vee KA/\langle\iota\rangle
by Mayer–Vietoris. Both source and target are simply connected, so the result follows by Whitehead’s theorem. Simple connectedness of
KA/\langle\iota\rangle
follows from
[
Arm65
]
.
∎

6.3.8
.

We will now prove formality of the space
KA/\langle\iota\rangle
, using a criterion due to Sullivan
[
Sul77
, Theorem 12.7]
. Let
V=\bigoplus_{n}V^{n}
be a graded
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space. For
q\in\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
we define a linear map
\phi_{q}:V\to V
by
\phi_{q}(x)=q^{n}x
, if
|x|=n
. Every map of this form is called a
grading automorphism
of
V
.

6.3.9 Theorem

(Sullivan)
.

Let
C
be a nilpotent commutative dg algebra over
\mathbf{Q}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

C
is formal.

(2)

Every automorphism of
H(C)
lifts to an automorphism of
C
.

(3)

Some grading automorphism
\phi_{q}
of
H(C)
, where
q\neq\pm 1
, lifts to an automorphism of
C
.

6.3.10 Proposition
.

The space
KA/\langle\iota\rangle
is formal in the sense of rational homotopy theory.

Proof.

Let the group
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
act on the graded vector space
A
by grading automorphisms. It induces an action of
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
on the space
KA
, and thus also an action of
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
on
H^{\bullet}(KA,\mathbf{Q})
. The action on
H^{\bullet}(KA,\mathbf{Q})=\mathrm{Sym}(A^{\vee})
is again by grading automorphisms. The action of
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
on
A
commutes with the involution
\iota
, so the action descends to an action of
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
on
KA/\langle\iota\rangle
, and since

H^{\bullet}(KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})\subset H^{\bullet}(KA,\mathbf{Q})

it again acts by grading automorphism. Thus, every grading automorphism of
H^{\bullet}(KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})
lifts to an automorphism of the
actual topological space

KA/\langle\iota\rangle
. In particular, every grading automorphism certainly lifts to an automorphism of the cochain algebra.
∎

6.3.11 Proposition
.

The cohomology ring
H^{\bullet}(KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})
is a Koszul algebra.

Proof.

The cohomology ring
H^{\bullet}(KA,\mathbf{Q})
is the symmetric algebra
\mathrm{Sym}(A^{\vee})=\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\mathrm{Sym}^{d}(A^{\vee})
. Depending on the parity of
A
this is either a polynomial ring or an exterior algebra (or a tensor product of the two). Either way, it is certainly a Koszul algebra. Now
H^{\bullet}(KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})
is the subalgebra
\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\mathrm{Sym}^{2d}(A^{\vee})
, i.e. the second Veronese subring of
\mathrm{Sym}(A^{\vee})
. But Veronese subalgebras of Koszul algebras are Koszul, by a theorem of Backelin and Fröberg
[
BF85
]
; see also
[
PP05
, Chapter 3, Proposition 2.2]
.
∎

6.3.12 Corollary
.

The space
W(A)
is Koszul.

Proof.

By
6.3.7
and
6.3.2
it suffices to show that
KA/\langle\iota\rangle
is a Koszul space. This is shown in
6.3.10
and
6.3.11
.
∎

6.4.
The homology of
\Omega^{2}_{0}W(A)

6.4.1 Theorem
.

The assignment
A\mapsto H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}_{0}W(A),\mathbf{Q})
is an analytic functor from
1
-connected graded vector spaces to graded vector spaces. As such, its Taylor series is given by

\mathbf{Exp}(z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{-r}h_{2r})-1).

Proof.

The calculation proceeds in multiple steps.

(i) The homology
H_{\bullet}(W(A),\mathbf{Q})
is a Koszul coalgebra. As such, it has a Hilbert–Poincaré series in the ring
\mathbf{Z}[[t,z]]
, where the variable
t
corresponds to length grading and the variable
z
corresponds to homological degree. But
A\mapsto H_{\bullet}(W(A),\mathbf{Q})
is in addition an analytic functor of
A
, and as such it has a Taylor series taking values in
\Lambda\widehat{\otimes}{\mathbf{Z}[[t,z]]}
. (This is the bigraded version of the construction for graded vector spaces explained in Section
2.4
.) Its Taylor series is

F(t,z)=tz^{2}+\sum_{r\geq 0}t^{r}h_{2r}.

Indeed, the Hilbert–Poincaré series of
H_{\bullet}(S^{2},\mathbf{Q})
is
1+tz^{2}
, which accounts for the first term, since
W(A)\simeq_{\mathbf{Q}}S^{2}\vee KA/\langle\iota\rangle
. The Hilbert–Poincaré series of
H_{\bullet}(KA,\mathbf{Q})\cong\mathrm{Sym}(A)
is
\mathbf{Exp}(th_{1})=\sum_{r\geq 0}t^{r}h_{r}
. Multiplication by
-1
in
A
acts on
H_{\bullet}(KA,\mathbf{Q})
via
h_{r}\mapsto(-1)^{r}h_{r}
, so the quotient coalgebra of coinvariants
H_{\bullet}(KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})
has Hilbert–Poincaré series
\sum_{r\geq 0}t^{r}h_{2r}
, the shift in the length grading arising from the fact that
H_{\bullet}(KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})
is instead cogenerated by
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(A)
.

(ii) Let
G(t,z)
be the Taylor series of the analytic functor

A\mapsto H_{\bullet}(\Omega W(A),\mathbf{Q}).

Using
6.3.4
and
6.2.8
, this Taylor series is given by

G(t,z)=\frac{1}{F(\tfrac{t}{z},z)}=\frac{1}{tz+\sum_{r\geq 0}t^{r}z^{-r}h_{2r}}.

The additional length grading will no longer play a role, and we thus set
t=1
, giving the Taylor series

G(z)=\frac{1}{z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{-r}h_{2r}}.

(iii) For a simply connected space
Y
,
H_{\bullet}(\Omega Y,\mathbf{Q})
is the symmetric algebra on
\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbf{Q}}(\Omega Y)
.
It follows that the analytic functor

A\mapsto\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbf{Q}}\,\Omega W(A)

has the Taylor series

\mathbf{Log}\,G(z)=-\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{-r}h_{2r}).

(iv) The analytic functor

A\mapsto\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbf{Q}}\,\Omega^{2}W(A)

differs from the previous one only by a degree shift, which corresponds to dividing by
-z
(the minus sign accounting for the Koszul sign rule, in accordance with the discussion in §
2.4.8
). Thus, its Taylor series is

z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{-r}h_{2r}).

Now

\mathbf{Log}(1+x)=x+\text{(higher order terms)}
, where the higher order terms are all products of Adams operations applied to
x
; hence the above Taylor series is of the form
1+\text{(higher order terms)}
, with the term
1
corresponding to the nontriviality of
\pi_{0}\,\Omega^{2}W(A)\cong\mathbf{Z}
. Note that all terms involving
z^{-r}h_{2r}
contribute only in positive homological degrees, despite the negative powers of
z
:
A
is concentrated in degree
\geq 2
, so
h_{2r}
is concentrated in degrees at least
4r
. For the homotopy groups of the base component we therefore see that
A\mapsto\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbf{Q}}\,\Omega_{0}^{2}W(A)
has Taylor series

z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{-r}h_{2r})-1,

so that the analytic functor

A\mapsto H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}_{0}W(A),\mathbf{Q})

has the Taylor series

\mathbf{Exp}(z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{-r}h_{2r})-1)

as claimed.
∎

7.
Symmetric function calculations

7.0.1
.

In this section we derive, by direct computations with symmetric functions, some consequences of the formula proven in
4.4.12
,

(2)

\sum\dim H_{k}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},V_{\lambda})(-z)^{k}s_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\mathbf{Exp}(z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1-h_{2}).

7.0.2 Theorem
.

If
k<|\lambda|/4
then
H_{k}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},V_{\lambda})=0
.

Proof.

We must show that every monomial
z^{k}s_{\lambda}
occurring nontrivially in the right-hand side of (
2
) satisfies the inequality
k\geq|\lambda|/4
. By
2.5.17
it suffices to prove this for
z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1-h_{2}
. Now since every monomial
z^{k}s_{\lambda}
occurring nontrivially in the sum
z+\sum_{k>0}z^{k}h_{2k}
satisfies the inequality
k<|\lambda|/2
, the same is true for the monomials occurring in
\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})
,
again by
2.5.17
. Hence, all terms of arity
\geq 4
in
z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1-h_{2}
must satisfy the desired inequality. To check the terms of low arity it suffices to compute:

z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(t+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2}k)-1-h_{2}=-z-zh_{2}+\text{ higher order terms},

where the higher order terms are of arity
\geq 4
or degree
\geq 2
with respect to
z
. The result follows.
∎

7.0.3
.

A very similar argument shows the following result, which confirms Conjecture 13.11 of
[
Ber09
]
. As stated in
[
Ber09
]
, this conjecture was observed and verified computationally for
|\lambda|\leq 30
(on the level of Euler characteristics). We remark that
[
Ber09
, Conjecture 13.11]
was stated in terms of the moduli space
H_{\infty}
of closed hyperelliptic surfaces, rather than
H_{\infty}^{0,1}
. Combining the below argument with the results of
Section

10
confirms the conjecture in its original form.

7.0.4 Theorem
.

If
\lambda_{1}>|\lambda|/2
then
H_{\bullet}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},V_{\lambda})=0
.

Proof.

We must show that every Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}
occurring nontrivially in the right-hand side of (
2
) satisfies the inequality
\ell(\lambda)\leq|\lambda|/2
. By
2.5.19
it suffices to prove this for
z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1-h_{2}
. By another application of
2.5.19
it suffices to prove this for
z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k}
. But this is clear.∎

7.0.5
.

The same strategy proves also a further vanishing theorem,
7.0.12
. To prove
7.0.12
we first need a bound on
\lambda_{1}
for partitions
\lambda
such that
s_{\lambda}
occurs in
\mathbf{Log}(\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k})
. We are grateful to Sheila Sundaram who explained the following argument for how to obtain such a bound.

7.0.6 Theorem

(Calderbank–Hanlon–Robinson)
.

\mathbf{Log}(\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k})=\sum_{m>0}(-1)^{m}\beta_{2m},

where
\beta_{2m}
denotes the character of
\Sigma_{2m}
acting on the top homology group of the poset of partitions of
\{1,\ldots,2m\}
with all blocks of even size.

7.0.7
.

Indeed, set
k=2
in
[
CHR86
, Corollary 4.7]
. See also
[
Sun94
, Example 1.6(iii)]
. The poset in question is Cohen–Macaulay, so the top homology group is its only nontrivial (reduced) homology group. The following proposition is explained in the start of Section 6 of
[
CHR86
]
.

7.0.8 Theorem

(Calderbank–Hanlon–Robinson)
.

For
m\geq 2
,

\frac{\partial\beta_{2m}}{\partial p_{1}}=s_{\delta_{m+1}/\delta_{m-1}},

where
\delta_{m}
denotes the staircase shape
(m-1,m-2,\ldots,1)
and
\delta_{m+1}/\delta_{m-1}
denotes a skew diagram.

7.0.9
.

A consequence is that if
\lambda\vdash 2m
is such that
s_{\lambda}
occurs with nonzero coefficient in
\beta_{2m}
, then
\lambda_{1}\leq m
, except for
\beta_{2}=s_{2}
. Indeed, the skew diagram
\delta_{m+1}/\delta_{m-1}
has exactly
m
columns, so
7.0.8
shows that if
s_{\mu}
occurs with nonzero coefficient in
\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}\beta_{2m}
then
\mu_{1}\leq m
. Pieri’s rule therefore shows that if
s_{\lambda}
occurs with nonzero coefficient in
\beta_{2m}
then
\lambda
satisfies the following condition: all partitions obtained from
\lambda
by removing a box have their largest entry bounded by
m=|\lambda|/2
. But now if
\lambda
has more than one entry, then removing a box from the last entry shows
\lambda_{1}\leq m
. If instead
\lambda=(2m)
has only one entry, then the condition is satisfied only for
\lambda=(2)\vdash 2
.

7.0.10 Lemma
.

Let
f,g\in\widehat{\Lambda}_{0}^{\mathsf{gr}}
. Then

f\circ(z+g)=\sum_{n\geq 0}z^{n}f^{(n)}\circ g

where
f^{(n)}=h_{n}^{\perp}f
.

Proof.

Note that

f\circ(z+g)=\sum_{\lambda}(s_{\lambda}\circ z)(s_{\lambda}^{\perp}f\circ g)=\sum_{n\geq 0}z^{n}(h_{n}^{\perp}f)\circ g
, since
s_{\lambda}\circ z=z^{n}
if
\lambda=(n)
, and
s_{\lambda}\circ z=0
otherwise.
∎

7.0.11 Lemma
.

Suppose that
z^{j}s_{\lambda}
occurs with nonzero coefficient in
\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k}z^{k})
. Then, with the sole exception of the monomial
zh_{2}
, one has the inequality
j-1\geq\lambda_{1}/2
.

Proof.

Let
L
be defined as in
2.3.6
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k}z^{k})

\displaystyle=L\circ(z+\sum_{k>0}h_{2k}z^{k})

by definition of
\mathbf{Log}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Log}(\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k}z^{k})+\sum_{n>0}z^{n}L^{(n)}\circ(\sum_{k>0}h_{2k}z^{k})

by
7.0.10

\displaystyle=\sum_{m>0}(-z)^{m}\beta_{2m}+\sum_{n>0}z^{n}L^{(n)}\circ(\sum_{k>0}h_{2k}z^{k})

by
7.0.6

The fact that the first term satisfies the conclusion of the lemma is in §
7.0.9
. As for the second, since every monomial
z^{j}s_{\lambda}
occurring in
\sum_{k>0}h_{2k}z^{k}
satisfies the inequality
j\geq\lambda_{1}/2
, the same will be true for
L^{(n)}\circ(\sum_{k>0}h_{2k}z^{k})
, by
2.5.20
.
∎

7.0.12 Theorem
.

If
k<\ell(\lambda)/2
then
H_{k}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},V_{\lambda})=0
.

Proof.

The theorem is equivalent to the assertion that if
z^{k}s_{\lambda}
occurs with nonzero coefficient in the right-hand side of (
2
), then
k\geq\lambda_{1}/2
. By
2.5.20
it suffices to prove this inequality for monomials occurring in
z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k}z^{k})-1-h_{2}
. But this is exactly the preceding lemma.
∎

7.0.13
.

Let us rewrite the generating series (
2
) in an alternative form.

7.0.14 Proposition
.

Let
i_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(n/d)t^{d}
denote the
n
th necklace polynomial. Then

\mathbf{Exp}(z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1)=(1-z)\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1+\frac{1}{1+z^{n}}\sum_{k>0}z^{nk}\psi_{n}(h_{2k}))^{i_{n}(z^{-1})}

Proof.

Note first that
z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1=z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(1-z^{2}+(1-z)\sum_{k>0}z^{k}h_{2k})
. Indeed, multiply by
z
and exponentiate, using
\mathbf{Exp}(-z)=1-z
. Using this identity and
2.3.12
, we get

\mathbf{Exp}(z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k})-1)=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-z^{2n}+(1-z^{n})\sum_{k>0}{z^{nk}}\psi_{n}(h_{2k}))^{i_{n}(z^{-1})}.

Combine this with the cyclotomic identity
(1-\alpha s)=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-s^{n})^{i_{n}(\alpha)}
, with
\alpha=z^{-1}
and
s=z^{2}
, and the result follows.
∎

7.0.15 Theorem
.

For any fixed
\lambda
, the sum
\sum\dim H_{k}(H_{\infty}^{1,0},S^{\lambda}(V))(-z)^{k}
is a rational function of
z
with all poles on the unit circle. In particular, the Betti numbers grow as a polynomial in
k
. Moreover, this polynomial is of degree exactly
|\lambda|/2-1
, unless
\lambda_{1}>|\lambda|/2
, in which case the homology vanishes by
7.0.4
.

Proof.

Expanding the infinite product appearing in
7.0.14
gives a sum of terms, each coefficient of which is a rational function whose denominator is a product of factors
(1+z^{n})
. The term with the largest number of such factors in the denominator occuring in arity
2N
is

\frac{1}{(1+z)^{N}}(zh_{2})^{N}\binom{z^{-1}}{N}.

A Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}
with
\lambda\vdash 2N
occurs in the expansion of
h_{2}^{N}
if and only if
\ell(\lambda)\leq N
.
∎

7.0.16 Remark
.

The polynomial growth of stable cohomology may be contrasted with the case of
M_{g}^{1}
. Indeed, already for constant coefficients we have that
H^{\bullet}(M_{\infty}^{1},\mathbf{Q})
is a polynomial algebra with a generator in each even degree; its Betti numbers grow like the number-theoretic partition function. The stable cohomology of
M_{g}^{1}
with coefficients in any local system
V_{\lambda}
is a free module over the cohomology with constant coefficients, and grows similarly.

8.
Framed
E_{2}
-structure and logarithmic geometry

8.1.
Motivic framed
E_{2}
-algebra structure

8.1.1
.

Let
q
be a prime power, and
\ell
an auxilliary prime not dividing
q
.

8.1.2
.

We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
the algebro-geometric moduli space of genus
g
curves with
n
distinct ordered marked points and a nonzero tangent vector at each marking. If
g\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
, then
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
is a smooth scheme over
\Spec\mathbf{Z}
(it is a scheme, not a stack, as no automorphism of a smooth curve of positive genus can fix a point and a nonzero tangent vector at that point). In all cases,
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
is a smooth stack of dimension
3g-3+2n
. For example,
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{1}
is the Artin stack
B\mathbb{G}_{a}
.

8.1.3
.

Recall that we defined
M_{g}^{n}
as the classifying space
B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(S_{g,n})
of an oriented genus
g
surface with
n
boundary components.
There is a homotopy equivalence
M_{g}^{n}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}(\mathbf{C})
, where by
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}(\mathbf{C})
we mean the analytification of
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
. By Artin’s comparison theorem, there is therefore an isomorphism

H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{sing}}(M_{g}^{n},\mathbf{Z})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\cong H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})

whereby the (co)homology of
M_{g}^{n}
carries the structure of
\ell
-adic Galois representation. Moreover, since
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
is the complement of a normal crossing divisor in a smooth proper stack over
\mathbf{Z}
(if
g+n>1
), we also have comparison isomorphisms

H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\cong H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})

between cohomology in characteristic zero and characteristic
p
. The result holds also in the exceptional cases
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{1}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{0}
by direct calculation; e.g. using that the cohomology of
B\mathbb{G}_{a}
is trivial.

8.1.4
.

We also have algebro-geometric avatars of the local systems
S^{\lambda}(V)
considered on the topological moduli spaces
M_{g}^{n}
(§
3.4.4
, §
3.5.11
). These local systems are naturally pulled back from the space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
of
n
-pointed curves (with no tangent vectors at markings). Suppose that
n>0
. Let
p:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
be the universal
n
-punctured curve, and define
\mathbb{V}=R^{1}p_{!}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
. (We suppress the dependence of
\mathbb{V}
on
\ell
for legibility.) Then
\mathbb{V}
is a local system of rank
2g+n-1
on
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
, fitting in a short exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}^{\oplus(n-1)}\to\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}^{0}\to 0

where
\mathbb{V}^{0}
is the pure sheaf of weight
1
defined as
R^{1}\overline{p}_{!}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
, where
\overline{p}:\overline{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
is the universal curve. The corresponding Betti local system over the complex numbers underlies an admissible variation of mixed Hodge structures, and may be identified with the local system
V
under the homotopy equivalence
M_{g}^{n}\cong\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}(\mathbf{C})
. There are similar comparison isomorphisms

H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{sing}}(M_{g}^{1},S^{\lambda}(V)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\cong H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{1}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))\cong H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{1}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))

by the same arguments as in the constant coefficient case, with the additional input into the comparison theorem that the sheaf
\mathbb{V}
is tamely ramified along the divisor at infinity. As in §
3.5.11
one may want to modify the above definition of
\mathbb{V}
in case
n=0
: a definition that works for any
n\geq 0
is to let
\mathbb{V}
denote the fiber of the trace map
Rp_{!}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}[1]\to\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(-1)[-1]
. However, we will have no reason to consider the case
n=0
in this section.

8.1.5
.

Using that
\coprod_{g\geq 0}M_{g}^{1}
is naturally a framed
E_{2}
-algebra (§
3.1.3
), there is an induced structure of Batalin–Vilkovisky (BV) algebra on its homology

\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}H_{\bullet}(M_{g}^{1},\mathbf{Q}),

since the homology of the framed little disk operad is the operad of BV algebras
[
Get94
]
. Similarly,
\coprod_{g\geq 0}M_{g}^{1}(KA)
is a framed
E_{2}
-algebra. By the calculation of its homology (
3.5.6
), or rather the homology of the base component of each space
M_{g}^{1}(KA)
, we see that there is a natural BV-algebra structure on

\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}(M_{g}^{1},S^{\lambda}(V[-1])).

(Let us suppose as in
3.5.6
that
A
is a graded vector space concentrated in degree
2
.) We now want to explain that this BV-structure is “motivic”. More precisely, we have the following theorem:

8.1.6 Theorem
.

Under the comparison isomorphism

\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{sing}}(M_{g}^{1},S^{\lambda}(V[-1])\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\cong\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{1}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}[-1]))

the natural BV-algebra structure on the left-hand side is compatible with the Galois action on the right-hand side, when the homology of the framed disk operad
FD
is given the Galois action which on
H_{k}(FD(n),\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
is given by the
k
th power of the cyclotomic character, i.e. a pure Tate motive of weight
-2k
.

8.1.7 Remark
.

There is for each
n
a homotopy equivalence
FD(n)\cong\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1}(\mathbf{C})
. On the face of it, this equivalence has no compatibility at all with operadic composition. Nevertheless, it produces the “correct” Galois action according to the theorem, as
H_{k}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
is a direct sum of copies of
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(k)
. Indeed,
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{n+1}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}\times\mathrm{PConf}_{n}(\mathbb{A}^{1})
, and complements of hyperplane arrangements have this purity property of their (co)homology
[
Leh92
,
Kim94
,
Sha93
]
.

8.1.8 Remark
.

A consequence of the theorem is that the transcendentally defined Harer stability map
H_{\bullet}(M_{g}^{1},S^{\lambda}(V))\to H_{\bullet}(M_{g+1}^{1},S^{\lambda}(V))
is compatible with Galois actions, since the Harer stabilization is multiplication in this BV-algebra by the fundamental class in
H_{0}(M_{1}^{1})
. This was previously known by
[
HL97
, Section 4]
, see also
[
Lev07
]
.

8.1.9 Remark
.

This result is closely related to conjectures of Cazanave
[
Caz09
, Section 3.5]
. It seems very likely that these conjectures, at least with “little disks” replaced by “framed little disks”, can be proved by similar reasoning.

8.1.10
.

8.1.6
will be deduced as a special case of a more general construction due to Vaintrob
[
Vai19
,
Vai21
]
. As explained in §
3.1.3
, the framed
E_{2}
-structure on
\coprod_{g\geq 0}M_{g}^{1}
can be seen as a consequence of the modular operad structure on the collection of spaces
\{M_{g}^{n}\}
, and the fact that
\{M_{0}^{n}\}
is a model for the cyclic operad of framed little disks. Vaintrob shows that there is a model for the surface operad
\{M_{g}^{n}\}
in terms of
logarithmic algebraic geometry
over
\Spec\mathbf{Z}
. The model in question was first considered by Kimura–Stasheff–Voronov
[
KSV95
]
(in genus zero, but the higher genus analogue is an immediate generalization). Kimura–Stasheff–Voronov described their spaces explicitly as real oriented blow-ups of complex algebraic varieties; the insight of Vaintrob is that these real oriented blow-ups may be realized explicitly as analytifications (which in the logarithmic setting are usually referred to as
Kato–Nakayama spaces
) of log schemes over
\mathbf{Z}
(or log stacks when
g>0
), from which it in particular follows that their homologies carry canonically a “motivic enhancement”: mixed Hodge structure,
\ell
-adic Galois representation, et cetera.

8.1.11
.

Since we do not expect all readers to be experts in logarithmic geometry, we include here a bare-bones approach to the theory sufficient for our purposes.

8.2.
Deligne–Faltings log structures

8.2.1
.

Foundations for the theory of logarithmic algebraic geometry were first given by Kato
[
Kat89
]
, building on ideas of Fontaine and Illusie. A closely related formalism was independently developed by Faltings
[
Fal90
]
, and a set-up equivalent to Faltings’s was also proposed in a letter from Deligne to Illusie. The definition of Deligne–Faltings is somewhat simpler than Kato’s: a log scheme in the sense of Deligne–Faltings is simply a scheme together with a finite collection of line bundles with sections. Kato refers to this notion as a
DF log scheme

[
Kat89
, Complement 1]
.

8.2.2
.

DF log schemes are equivalent to a full subcategory of Kato log schemes; specifically, the subcategory of locally free Kato log schemes which admit a global chart. All log schemes in this paper will be naturally presented by Deligne–Faltings data, and it will be convenient to work within this formalism rather than Kato’s. From now on, we omit the modifier “DF”, so that the phrase “log scheme” or “log structure” will always be taken to mean “DF log scheme” or “DF log structure”. A remark is that Borne and Vistoli
[
BV12
]
define a strictly more general notion of Deligne–Faltings logarithmic structure on a scheme than the one used here.

8.2.3 Definition
.

Let
X
be a scheme. A
log structure
on
X
is a
k
-tuple
\mathfrak{L}=(\sigma_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\to L_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of line bundles on
X
with sections, for some
k\geq 0
.

8.2.4
.

The collection of all log structures on
X
form a category: a morphism of log structures
(\sigma_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\to L_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\longrightarrow(\tau_{j}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\to M_{j})_{j=1}^{l}
consists of natural numbers
e_{ij}
,
1\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq j\leq l
, together with isomorphisms

L_{i}\cong\bigotimes_{j=1}^{l}M_{j}^{\otimes e_{ij}}

under which the section
\sigma_{i}
is identified with the corresponding tensor product of the sections
\tau_{j}
.

8.2.5 Remark
.

The categorically minded reader may prefer the following description of the category of log structures. Let
\mathbf{N}
be the category whose objects are nonnegative integers, whose only morphisms are identities, considered as a symmetric monoidal category under addition. Let
\mathfrak{Div}(X)
be the category of line bundles on
X
equipped with a section, considered as a symmetric monoidal category under tensor product. Then a log structure on
X
is a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathbf{N}^{k}\to\mathfrak{Div}(X)
for some
k
, and a morphism of such log structures is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{N}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{N}^{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Div}(X)}

of strong symmetric monoidal functors commuting up to a specified natural transformation.

8.2.6
.

A morphism of schemes
f\colon X\to Y
induces an evident functor from the category of log structures on
Y
to the category of log structures on
X
, which we write as
\mathfrak{L}\mapsto f^{\ast}\mathfrak{L}
.

8.2.7 Definition
.

A
log scheme
is a scheme equipped with a log structure. We typically write log schemes in a sans-serif font, so that
\mathsf{X}
might denote a log scheme and
X
its underlying scheme. A morphism of log schemes
\mathsf{X}=(X,\mathfrak{L})\to\mathsf{Y}=(Y,\mathfrak{M})
is a morphism of underlying schemes
f:X\to Y
and a morphism of log structures
f^{\ast}\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{L}
. Such a morphism of log schemes is said to be
strict
if
f^{\ast}\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{L}
is an isomorphism of log structures.

8.2.8 Remark
.

We will freely use also the evident generalization of a
log stack
.

8.3.
Real oriented blow-up

8.3.1
.

The analytifications of log schemes are naturally described in terms of the
real oriented blow-up
. In this section we give a self-contained and elementary treatment of the real oriented blow-up in the generality needed here.

8.3.2
.

Let us first discuss the “standard” blow-up of nonsingular varieties. Recall that if
Z
is a smooth complete intersection of codimension
k
in a smooth variety
X
, with defining equations
f_{1}=f_{2}=\ldots=f_{k}=0
, then the blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{Z}\,X
can be written in equations by the formula

\{(x,[w_{1}:\ldots:w_{k}])\in X\times\mathbb{P}^{k-1}:f_{i}(x)w_{j}=f_{j}(x)w_{i}\text{ for all }i,j\}.

It is clear from the formula that the blow-up comes with a map
\mathrm{Bl}_{Z}\,X\to X
, which is an isomorphism over
X\setminus Z
, and the fiber over a point of
Z
is a projective space
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
.

8.3.3
.

More generally if
f\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{k}
is any morphism, with coordinate functions
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
then we may use the same formula to define

\mathrm{Bl}_{f}\,X=\{(x,[w_{1}:\ldots:w_{k}])\in X\times\mathbb{P}^{k-1}:f_{i}(x)w_{j}=f_{j}(x)w_{i}\text{ for all }i,j\}.

The notation is abusive, since in this level of generality,
\mathrm{Bl}_{f}\,X
will perhaps not deserve to be called a “blow-up”. For example, if
f=0
is identically zero, then
\mathrm{Bl}_{0}\,X\cong X\times\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
. A correct statement is that
\mathrm{Bl}_{f}\,X
is the dominant transform of the graph of
f
when we blow up
X\times\mathbb{A}^{k}
along
X\times\{0\}
; recall that the dominant transform equals the strict transform except if there is an irreducible component contained in the center of the blow-up, in which case the dominant transform includes the full inverse image of this component, whereas the strict transform of this component is empty.

8.3.4
.

Even more generally, suppose that
E\to X
is a rank
k
vector bundle, and
\sigma\colon X\to E
is a section. We define

\mathrm{Bl}_{\sigma}\,X
to be the dominant transform of the graph of
\sigma
when we blow up the total space of
E
along the zero section. We obtain a proper map
\mathrm{Bl}_{\sigma}\,X\to X
which is an isomorphism away from the zero-set of
\sigma
, and the fiber over a point on which
\sigma
vanishes is a projective space
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
.

8.3.5
.

The construction just defined is functorial in a natural way: given a vector bundle
E\to Y
with a section
\sigma
, and a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
, we get a section
f^{\ast}\sigma
of the pullback bundle
f^{\ast}E
, and there is a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bl}_{f^{\ast}\sigma}\,X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bl}_{\sigma}\,Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}

8.3.6
.

In the definition we used very little of the algebraic structure. In fact the exact same formula makes perfect sense for, say, a topological space
X
equipped with a continuous map
f:X\to\mathbf{C}^{k}
.
Moreover, if instead we are given a topological space
X
with a continuous map
f:X\to\mathbf{R}^{k}
, then we can write down an exactly analogous space, replacing complex projective space with real projective space. But in the real setting we also have the possibility of taking the quotient by
\mathbf{R}_{>0}
instead of
\mathbf{R}^{\ast}
, so to speak, giving a subset of
X\times S^{k-1}
. This is called the
real oriented blow-up
. To be precise, let us identify
S^{k-1}
with
(\mathbf{R}^{k}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbf{R}_{>0}
, which lets us write a point of
S^{k-1}
in terms of real homogeneous coordinates
[w_{1}:\ldots:w_{k}]
, well-defined up to simultaneous rescaling by a positive real number. Given a topological space
X
and a continuous function
f\colon X\to\mathbf{R}^{k}
, with components
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
, the real-oriented blow-up of
X
is defined as

\mathrm{Bl}^{\mathbf{R}}_{f}\,X:=\{(x,[w_{1}:\ldots:w_{k}])\in X\times S^{k-1}:\text{there exists $\alpha>0$ such that }f_{i}(x)=\alpha w_{i}\text{ for all }i\}.

Given a rank
k
real vector bundle
E\to X
with a section
\sigma
, we can in exactly the same way define a real oriented blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{\sigma}^{\mathbf{R}}\,X
over
X
. For example, we may choose an open cover of
X
trivializing the bundle. Over each open set we may form the real oriented blow-up as just defined, and these blow-ups then glue together using the gluing data for the original vector bundle.

8.3.7
.

The construction can be done with more care: if
X
and
f
are real analytic then
\mathrm{Bl}_{f}^{\mathbf{R}}\,X
can be given a real analytic structure, and one can define more generally a real oriented blow-up in a real analytic submanifold, but we will have no need to do so.

8.3.8
.

In fact, the only situation that will be used in this paper is the following:
X
is a complex algebraic variety,
L
is an algebraic line bundle on
X
, and
\sigma
a section of
L
. Then we may consider
L
as a rank two real vector bundle and form the real oriented blowup
\mathrm{Bl}^{\mathbf{R}}_{\sigma}\,X
.

8.4.
Kato-Nakayama space

8.4.1
.

We can now define the
Kato–Nakayama space
(or “analytification”) of a log scheme over
\mathbf{C}
. Given a complex log scheme
\mathsf{X}=(X,\mathfrak{L})
, with
\mathfrak{L}=(\sigma_{i}:\mathcal{O}_{X}\to L_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
, we define
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
to be the topological space given by the iterated real blow-up

\mathrm{Bl}^{\mathbf{R}}_{\sigma_{k}}\,\ldots\mathrm{Bl}^{\mathbf{R}}_{\sigma_{1}}\,X(\mathbf{C}).

That is, we consider
L_{1}
as a real rank two vector bundle on
X(\mathbf{C})
and carry out the real oriented blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}^{\mathbf{R}}_{\sigma_{1}}
, we pull back the bundle
L_{2}
to the blow-up and carry out the blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}^{\mathbf{R}}_{\sigma_{2}}
, etc. The same recipe applied to a complex log stack produces a topological stack.

8.4.2
.

A more symmetric construction of the Kato–Nakayama space
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is to consider the total space of the direct sum
L_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus L_{k}
, and the arrangement of subvarieties given by the vanishing of each of the “coordinate functions”. Then
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is the dominant transform of the image of the section
\sigma_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\sigma_{k}
when we take the real oriented blow-up of this arrangement, in the sense of Li
[
Li09
]
; as in
[
Li09
]
the result is independent of the order in which we carry out the blow-up.

8.4.3
.

While it is well known that
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is a real oriented blow-up, we do not know a reference for the exact description of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
given here. One way to see it is as follows. If
\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
is a strict morphism of complex log schemes or log stacks, then there is a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{KN}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\mathbf{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\mathbf{C}).}

By construction every log scheme admits a strict morphism to the log stack
[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]^{k}
for some
k
; this is the stack parametrizing a
k
-tuple of line bundles with sections, and it has a tautologically defined log structure. Thus we only need to show that the description given here reduces to the usual Kato–Nakayama topological stack in the case of
[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]^{k}
. Since the Kato–Nakayama functor commutes with products, we only need to treat the case
k=1
. The total space of the tautological complex line bundle on
[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
is just
\mathbf{C}
, and its zero section is the origin, so the Kato–Nakayama space in this situation is the quotient of the real oriented blow-up of
\mathbf{C}
at the origin by the action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, as it should be.

8.4.4 Example
.

Let
L\to X
be a complex line bundle, and let
\mathsf{X}
denote the log scheme defined by the zero section of this bundle. Then
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is diffeomorphic to the unit circle bundle over
X(\mathbf{C})
with respect to some bundle metric on
L
.

8.4.5 Example
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex variety,
D\subset X
a smooth divisor. Let
\mathsf{X}
be the log scheme defined by the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
with its natural section
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
. Then
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is a manifold with boundary, diffeomorphic to the complement of a tubular neighborhood of
D
in
X
.

8.4.6 Example
.

Keep the notation of the preceding example. Since
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
restricted to
D
is the normal bundle of
D
, there is a map of log schemes
\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{X}
, where
\mathsf{D}
is the log structure on
D
defined by the normal bundle and its zero section. Taking Kato–Nakayama spaces,
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is a manifold with boundary, and the map
\mathsf{D}^{\mathrm{KN}}\to\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is the inclusion of the boundary.

8.4.7 Example
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex variety, and
D\subset X
a normal crossing divisor, with components
D=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}
. Let
\mathsf{X}
be the log scheme defined by the collection of line bundles-with-sections
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i})
. Then
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}
is naturally a manifold with corners, diffeomorphic to the complement of the union of a tubular neighborhood around each
D_{i}
. If
x\in X(\mathbf{C})
lies in
r
of the components
D_{i}
, then the fiber of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}}\to X(\mathbf{C})
is a product of
r
circles.

8.5.
(Co)homology of log schemes

8.5.1
.

Having defined the analytification of a complex log scheme, we in particular have a singular (or “Betti”) cohomology theory defined by
H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{sing}}(\mathsf{X}^{\mathrm{KN}},\mathbf{Z})
. It is then natural to ask whether we can also define étale, algebraic de Rham, or crystalline cohomology of log schemes, with expected comparison isomorphisms, whether the Betti cohomology underlies a mixed Hodge structure, and so on and so forth. But we will not discuss the theory of log motives (although see e.g.
[
Shu22
,
BPØ22
]
); what will be relevant for us in this paper is simply having a well-behaved theory of étale cohomology.

8.5.2
.

The theory of logarithmic étale cohomology is largely parallel to the classical theory. The role of the étale site is played by the
Kummer-étale
site. We recommend
[
Ill02
]
and the original references
[
KN99
,
Nak97
]
. Let us now explain how these étale cohomology groups behave in the simplest examples. The cases of interest to us will be no more complicated.

8.5.3
.

Let us consider the situations of Examples
8.4.4
–
8.4.6
. In Example
8.4.5
, we may consider the morphism
(X\setminus D)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{X}
, where
X\setminus D
has the trivial log structure. This map induces a homotopy equivalence of Kato–Nakayama spaces, and for any reasonable cohomology theory one should have
H^{\bullet}(X\setminus D)\cong H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{X})
, which uniquely “pins down” the motivic structure of
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{X})
. Similarly, in the setting of Example
8.4.4
we must have
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{X})\cong H^{\bullet}(L^{\ast})
