, where
L^{\ast}
denotes the complement of the zero-section in the total space of
L
, equipped with the trivial log structure: indeed, this follows from the previous discussion and the fact that
\mathsf{X}
includes into the total space of
L
, equipped with the log structure induced from the divisor given by the zero-section, such that the map on Kato–Nakayama spaces is a homotopy equivalence.

8.5.4
.

There is also a good theory of local systems on log schemes, and a comparison with locally constant sheaves on the Kummer-étale site. When
\mathsf{X}
is a log scheme defined by a normal crossing divisor
D
on a smooth variety
X
, then local systems on
\mathsf{X}
are equivalent to local systems on
X\setminus D
that are tamely ramified along
D
.

8.6.
Log models of moduli spaces of surfaces with boundary (after D. Vaintrob)

8.6.1
.

Suppose that
2g-2+n>0
. Let
\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}
be the following log stack: the underlying stack is
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, and the log structure is the one defined by the normal crossing divisor given by the complement of
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, together with the zero sections of the
n
tangent line bundles
\mathbb{T}_{i}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, whose fiber over a moduli point
[C,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is the tangent space of
C
at the point
x_{i}
. (Thus
\mathbb{T}_{i}
is the dual of the line bundle which defines the usual
\psi
-class
\psi_{i}
.) Following Vaintrob we call
\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}
the moduli of
framed log curves
.

8.6.2
.

Consider the gluing map
f:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n+1}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g^{\prime},n^{\prime}+1}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g+g^{\prime},n+n^{\prime}}
. The normal bundle of
f
is the tensor product of the tangent line bundles at the two points being glued together
[
ACG11
, Chapter XIII, §3]
. It follows that the gluing is compatible with the log structure just defined, producing a map

\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n+1}\times\mathsf{M}_{g^{\prime}}^{n^{\prime}+1}\to\mathsf{M}_{g+g^{\prime}}^{n+n^{\prime}}.

These give the collection
\{\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}\}
the structure of a modular operad in log stacks over
\mathbf{Z}
.

8.6.3
.

The Kato–Nakayama space
(\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n})^{\mathrm{KN}}
is homotopy equivalent to
M_{g}^{n}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}(\mathbf{C})
. Informally speaking, the part of the log structure given by the boundary of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
has the effect of deleting the boundary, and the part of the log structure given by the
n
tangent line bundles has the effect of taking the product of the
n
unit circle bundles associated with these line bundles over
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
. More is true: the collection
\{(\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n})^{\mathrm{KN}}\}
form a topological modular operad, isomorphic to the Kimura–Stasheff–Voronov model
[
KSV95
]
for the surface operad
\{M_{g}^{n}\}
. Indeed, Kimura–Stasheff–Voronov explicitly describe their model as a real oriented blow-up in a manner similar to how we defined Kato–Nakayama spaces.

8.6.4
.

We claim now that
8.1.6
is a consequence of the construction of the logarithmic model
\{\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}\}
of the surface operad. Indeed:
\coprod_{g\geq 1}\mathsf{M}_{g}^{1}
is an algebra over the cyclic operad
\{\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n}\}
in log stacks. Taking Kato–Nakayama spaces we obtain a model for the algebra
\coprod_{g\geq 1}M_{g}^{1}
over the topological framed
E_{2}
-operad
\{M_{0}^{n}\}
, giving the left-hand side of the isomorphism in
8.1.6
; taking étale homology we obtain the right-hand side, with its natural Galois action. Strictly speaking, what we have said is an argument for the homology with constant coefficients, but the gluing maps for the spaces
\{\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}\}
are compatible with the algebro-geometric local systems
\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}
in the same way as the corresponding topological local systems on the spaces
\{M_{g}^{n}\}
.

8.6.5 Remark
.

The argument in the previous paragraph is in some respects unsatisfactory. It would be more appealing to be able to directly construct a log stack
\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}(KA)
with a morphism of modular operads
\{\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}\}\to\{\mathsf{M}_{g}^{n}(KA)\}
, which upon taking analytifications produces a model for
\{M_{g}^{n}\}\to\{M_{g}^{n}(KA)\}
. Considering moduli spaces of curves with a map to a target space, one is naturally led to consider the Kontsevich space of stable maps. In the simple case of genus zero maps to a
convex
smooth projective variety
X
, we have a smooth stack of stable maps
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n}(X)=\coprod_{\beta}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n}(X,\beta)
. Its boundary is a normal crossing divisor, and we can give it a log structure producing a log stack
\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n}(X)
with a map of modular operads
\{\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n}\}\to\{\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n}(X)\}
; this accomplishes roughly what we want, except we are considering an algebraic mapping space as opposed to the space of all continuous maps. In order to model the space
M_{g}^{n}(KA)
we would need to work in arbitrary genus, and more critically it seems that we would need to be able to take for the target space
X
a
higher stack
, more precisely a product of affine stacks
K(\mathbb{G}_{a},n)
, as considered by Toën
[
Toë06
]
. However, to our knowledge nobody has considered a theory of stable maps into such a “very stacky” target in the literature. It would be interesting to be able to formulate the arguments in terms of such a space. Similarly, in the following section it would be most natural to formulate the arguments in terms of “twisted”
genus zero
stable maps to a product of quotient stacks
[K(\mathbb{G}_{a},n)/\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}]
.

9.
Hyperelliptic curves and computation of Galois action

9.1.
Motivic framed
E_{2}
-structure in the hyperelliptic case

9.1.1
.

All of what we did in the previous section admits a hyperelliptic analogue. As the results and arguments involve no new ingredients except replacing the spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
with the admissible cover spaces of Abramovich–Corti–Vistoli
[
ACV03
]
, we permit ourselves to be brief.

9.1.2
.

We denote by
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}
the moduli stack of smooth hyperelliptic curves of genus
g
, equipped with
n
distinct ordered marked Weierstrass points, and also
m
distinct ordered marked non-Weierstrass points, none of which are conjugate under the hyperelliptic involution, and at each marking a nonzero tangent vector. This is a smooth stack over
\Spec\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
. Its analytification
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}(\mathbf{C})
has the homotopy type of the topological moduli space
H_{g}^{n,m}
of hyperelliptic surfaces defined in §
4.1.5
.

9.1.3
.

From the work of Abramovich–Corti–Vistoli
[
ACV03
]
one obtains a smooth modular compactification of
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}
over
\Spec\mathbf{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]
such that the complement is a normal crossing divisor. For any odd
q
one therefore has comparison isomorphisms

H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{sing}}(H_{g}^{n,m},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\cong H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\cong H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}),

and similarly for coefficients in the local systems
S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V})
. Here
\mathbb{V}
denotes the local system on
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}
of rank
2g+n+2m-1
defined as in §
8.1.4
, given by
R^{1}p_{!}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
where
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{H}_{g}^{n,m}
is the universal
(n+2m)
-punctured curve. We will only consider the local system
\mathbb{V}
in the cases
(n,m)=(1,0)
and
(n,m)=(0,1)
.

9.1.4
.

Now as before there is a natural BV-algebra structure on

\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}(H_{g}^{0,1},S^{\lambda}(V[-1])),

since this is the homology of the framed
E_{2}
-algebra
\coprod_{g\geq 0}H_{g}^{0,1}(KA)
. Again we claim that the BV-algebra structure is compatible with the Galois action under the natural comparison isomorphism:

9.1.5 Theorem
.

Under the comparison isomorphism

\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{sing}}(H_{g}^{0,1},S^{\lambda}(V[-1])\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\cong\bigoplus_{g\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}[-1]))

the natural BV-algebra structure on the left-hand side is compatible with the Galois action on the right-hand side, when the homology of the framed disk operad
FD
is given the Galois action which on
H_{k}(FD(n),\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
is given by the
k
th power of the cyclotomic character, i.e. a pure Tate motive of weight
-2k
.

9.1.6
.

As before, we deduce this from the existence of a suitable model of the topological cyclic operad
\{H_{g}^{n,m}\}
(see §
4.2.6
) in terms of logarithmic algebraic geometry, and the fact that the framed
E_{2}
-structure on
\coprod_{g\geq 0}H_{g}^{0,1}
can be understood as an instance of this operadic structure. Explicitly, we define
\mathsf{H}_{g}^{n,m}
to be the following log stack: the underlying stack is the Abramovich–Corti–Vistoli compactification
[
ACV03
]
of the moduli space
\mathcal{H}_{g,n,m}
of genus
g
hyperelliptic curves with
n
ordered Weierstrass points and
m
ordered non-Weierstrass points (none of which are conjugate under the hyperelliptic involution). The operadic structure on these spaces is described in
[
Pet13
]
. We give it the log structure induced by the normal crossing boundary divisor, and the zero sections of the
(n+m)
tangent line bundles at the marked points. The spaces
\{\mathsf{H}_{g}^{n,m}\}
form a two-colored cyclic operad in log stacks, and taking Kato–Nakayama spaces gives a model for the operad
\{H_{g}^{n,m}\}
.

9.2.
Gerstenhaber indecomposables

9.2.1
.

The goal of this section is to prove the following purity result:

9.2.2 Theorem
.

The Galois representation on the stable homology groups

H_{k}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{0,1}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))

is pure Tate of weight
-2k+|\lambda|
.

9.2.3
.

To this end, we consider the group-completion of the framed
E_{2}
-algebra
\coprod_{g\geq 0}H_{g}^{0,1}(KA)
. By the group-completion theorem its homology is given by

\bigoplus_{g\in\mathbf{Z}}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},S^{\lambda}(V[-1])).

On the other hand, we identified in
5.0.3
this group completion as the loop space
\Omega^{2}W(A)
. From the Galois action on the homology of
H_{g}^{0,1}
described in the preceding subsection, it follows that we have a natural Galois action on the homology of
\Omega^{2}W(A)
, say with
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-coefficients. From
9.1.5
it follows that this Galois action is compatible with the BV-algebra structure on the homology of
\Omega^{2}W(A)
. In fact, the BV-operator will play no role in the argument, and we consider
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}W(A),\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
as a Gerstenhaber algebra. Since the Galois action is compatible with the Gerstenhaber algebra structure, we see that in order to determine the Galois action on
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}W(A),\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
it suffices to determine the Galois action on the Gerstenhaber indecomposables.

9.2.4 Theorem
.

The indecomposables in the Gerstenhaber algebra

H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}W(A),\mathbf{Q})\cong\bigoplus_{g\in\mathbf{Z}}\bigoplus_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}(A)\otimes H_{\bullet}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},S^{\lambda}(V[-1]))

can be identified with the following three summands:

•

(g,\lambda)=(1,0)
; the summand
\mathbf{Q}\otimes H_{0}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},\mathbf{Q})
.

•

(g,\lambda)=(-1,0)
; the summand
\mathbf{Q}\otimes H_{0}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},\mathbf{Q})
.

•

(g,\lambda)=(0,[2])
; the summand
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(A)\otimes H_{0}(H_{\infty}^{0,1},\wedge^{2}(V))[-2]
.

9.2.5
.

The arithmetic
9.2.2
is a direct consequence of the purely topological
9.2.4
. Indeed, after
9.2.4
it suffices to compute the Galois action on the stable homology in degree
0
. But this is obvious: the action is trivial with trivial coefficients, and pure Tate of weight
2
on the homology with coefficients in
\wedge^{2}\mathbb{V}
, where the nonzero homology arises since the symplectic form
5
5

5

The word “symplectic form” is strictly speaking incorrect here:
\mathbb{V}
is a symplectic vector space only when
(n,m)=(1,0)
, and we are considering the case
(n,m)=(0,1)
, which puts us in the situation of Proctor’s odd symplectic groups
[
Pro88
]
.
defines a direct summand
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(-1)\subset\wedge^{2}\mathbb{V}
. Then the homology in any other degree and for any other
\lambda
is determined by the compatibility with Galois action, and that the Gerstenhaber operad has the Galois action which in homological degree
k
is pure Tate of weight
-2k
.

9.2.6
.

The proof of
9.2.4
uses Koszul duality theory for Gerstenhaber algebras. Recall the following theorem of Berglund
[
Ber14
]
:

9.2.7 Theorem

(Berglund)
.

Let
X
be an
n
-connected Koszul space of finite type. The commutative algebra
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
, considered as a Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra with trivial bracket, is Koszul as a Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra. Its Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra is
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{n}X,\mathbf{Q})
.

9.2.8 Remark
.

In our earlier discussions on Koszul duality we formulated it as a duality between algebras and coalgebras, but here we have combined with the linear dual to obtain a duality between algebras and algebras. It is only for this reason we need
X
to be of finite type. We work with this formulation to be consistent with Berglund’s paper, and with Sullivan’s work on rational homotopy theory.

9.2.9
.

In formulating this theorem we use that the Gerstenhaber operad is self-dual up to a degree shift; after making this shift by
n
, any Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra has a Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra. We will need a modest generalization of Berglund’s result to the case that
X
is merely
(n-1)
-connected.

9.2.10 Definition
.

Let
A_{\bullet}
be a nonnegatively graded algebra of the form
A_{+}\otimes A_{0}
, with
A_{+}
connected and
A_{0}
a polynomial ring concentrated in degree
0
. A
group-completion
of
A
is a localization of the form
S^{-1}A
, where
S
is a minimal generating set of
A_{0}
.

9.2.11 Theorem
.

Let
X
be an
(n-1)
-connected Koszul space of finite type. The commutative algebra
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
, considered as a Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra with trivial bracket, is Koszul. The homology
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{n}X,\mathbf{Q})
is a group-completion of the Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra.

Proof.

This follows from the arguments of Berglund
[
Ber14
, Section 4]
. Berglund shows in general for a simply connected Koszul space
X
that the Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra of
H^{\bullet}(X,\mathbf{Q})
is the free algebra on the rational homotopy groups of
X
, shifted down
n
degrees. When
X
is
n
-connected, this free algebra is precisely
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{n}X,\mathbf{Q})
. When
X
is merely
(n-1)
-connected, this fails due to nontriviality of
\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}X)
:
H_{0}(\Omega^{n}X,\mathbf{Q})
is the Laurent polynomial algebra on
\pi_{n}^{\mathbf{Q}}(X)
, not the polynomial algebra. But this is the only difference, and forming the group-completion in the Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra produces
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{n}X,\mathbf{Q})
.
∎

9.2.12 Remark
.

It is instructive to consider the case
n=2
and
X=S^{2}
. One may compute
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}_{0}S^{2},\mathbf{Q})
in two different ways. One way is to use that the
2
-connected cover of
S^{2}
is
S^{3}
, so
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}_{0}S^{2},\mathbf{Q})\cong H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}S^{3},\mathbf{Q})
, which by
9.2.7
is a free Gerstenhaber algebra on a single generator
y
in degree
1
. Alternatively,
9.2.11
identifies
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}S^{2},\mathbf{Q})
with the group-completion of the free Gerstenhaber algebra on a single generator
x
in degree
0
. We may identify
H_{\bullet}(\Omega_{0}^{2}S^{2},\mathbf{Q})
with the Gerstenhaber subalgebra generated by the bracket
[x,x]=y
.

9.2.13
.

The crucial fact is now that Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebras have dual quadratic presentations
[
Ber14
, Theorem 2.11]
. If
D
is the space of indecomposables in a Koszul Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra, then the indecomposables in the Koszul dual Gerstenhaber
n
-algebra are given by
D^{\vee}[n]
.

Proof of
9.2.4
.

We compute the Gerstenhaber indecomposables in
H_{\bullet}(\Omega^{2}W(A),\mathbf{Q})
. By
9.2.11
and §
9.2.13
this reduces to computing the indecomposables in the algebra

H^{\bullet}(W(A),\mathbf{Q})\cong H^{\bullet}(S^{2}\vee KA/\langle\iota\rangle,\mathbf{Q})

which are evidently
\mathbf{Q}[-2]
(the fundamental class of the
2
-sphere) and
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(A^{\vee})
(which generates the cohomology ring of
KA/\langle\iota\rangle)
. In the homology of
\Omega^{2}W(A)
these two summands correspond to the first and third summand enumerated in
9.2.4
. The second summand of
9.2.4
arises by inverting the first summand in the process of group-completing. ∎

10.
Closed surfaces

10.0.1
.

In this section we will extend the homological stability results to moduli spaces of closed surfaces. It will be convenient at this point to switch from homology to cohomology. Let
\mathcal{H}_{g}
be the moduli stack of hyperelliptic curves of genus
g\geq 2
, and let
\mathbb{H}
be the standard rank
2g
local system on
\mathcal{H}_{g}
, i.e.
R^{1}p_{\ast}\mathbf{Q}
, where
p:\mathcal{C}_{g}\to\mathcal{H}_{g}
is the universal curve. We opt to denote this local system
\mathbb{H}
rather than
\mathbb{V}
for two reasons:

(1)

In the arguments we will consider the pullbacks of
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1}
. On
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1}
there is a short exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{Q}\to\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{H}\to 0

relating the local system that we have so far denoted
\mathbb{V}
with the pullback of
\mathbb{H}
, and it will be important to distinguish the two notationally.

(2)

In §
3.5.11
and §
8.1.4
we gave a definition of a local system
6
6

6

More precisely, a cohomologically locally constant complex in
D^{b}(\mathcal{M}_{g})
.

\mathbb{V}
on
\mathcal{M}_{g}
, whose restriction to
\mathcal{H}_{g}
is not the same as
\mathbb{H}
.

In this section we calculate the cohomology groups

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))

in a stable range, with their Galois action. In particular, the cohomology stabilizes in the sense that there are isomorphisms

H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g+1},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))

for
g\gg k
, in spite of the fact that there are no natural stabilization maps when we work with surfaces without boundary. This fact was previously proven by an indirect argument in
[
MPP19
]
.

10.0.2
.

Let first
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}
resp.
\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1}
denote the moduli spaces of hyperelliptic curves with a marked Weierstrass point, resp. a marked non-Weierstrass point. By keeping track of Galois actions in
4.1.13
, one sees that for any
\ell
-adic sheaf
\mathbb{F}
on
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}
resp.
\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1}
, there are isomorphisms

H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},\mathbb{F})=H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},\mathbb{F})\oplus H^{k-1}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},\mathbb{F})(-1)

and

H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1},\mathbb{F})=H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1},\mathbb{F})\oplus H^{k-1}(\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1},\mathbb{F})(-1)

for any
k
.

10.0.3 Proposition
.

If
k\leq\frac{2g-1-|\lambda|}{2}
, then
H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
is pure Tate of weight
2k+|\lambda|
.

Proof.

Since the forgetful map
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}\to\mathcal{H}_{g}
is finite,
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\to H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
is injective. (The trace map gives a left inverse.) But
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
injects into
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
by
4.1.13
, and we proved in
9.2.2
that the latter is pure Tate of this weight in a stable range. (Note that
\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{H}
on
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}
, as opposed to on
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1}
.)
∎

10.0.4
.

Once we are armed with this purity result, it suffices to find an expression for the weight-graded Euler characteristic of the homology in a stable range. We set

\Phi_{g}=\sum_{k}\sum_{i}\sum_{\lambda}(-1)^{k}z^{i/2}s_{\lambda}\dim\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{i}H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))

and define similarly generating series

\Phi_{g}^{1,0}=\sum_{k}\sum_{i}\sum_{\lambda}(-1)^{k}z^{i/2}s_{\lambda}\dim\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{i}H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))

and

\Phi_{g}^{0,1}=\sum_{k}\sum_{i}\sum_{\lambda}(-1)^{k}z^{i/2}s_{\lambda}\dim\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{i}H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V})).

We may also define evident analogues
\Phi_{g,1,0}
and
\Phi_{g,0,1}
by replacing
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}
by
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}
, etc.  but from §
10.0.2
we see that

\Phi_{g,1,0}=\frac{1}{1-z}\Phi_{g}^{1,0},\qquad\Phi_{g,0,1}=\frac{1}{1-z}\Phi_{g}^{0,1}.

We have similarly the stable versions of these generating series when
g=\infty
. We have not yet proven that
\Phi_{\infty}
is well-defined, but this will be deduced shortly.

10.0.5 Proposition
.

For every
g
, there is an identity

(1-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}+z)\Phi_{g}=z\Phi_{g,1,0}+\sum_{j\geq 0}(-1)^{j}e_{j}\Phi_{g,0,1},

where
\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}
is the partial derivative with respect to
p_{1}=h_{1}=e_{1}
, and
e_{j}
denotes the
j
th elementary symmetric polynomial.

Proof.

We argue that both sides of the identity are given by

\sum_{k}\sum_{i}\sum_{\lambda}(-1)^{k}z^{i/2}s_{\lambda}\dim\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{i}H^{k}(\mathcal{C}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H})),

where
\mathcal{C}_{g}\to\mathcal{H}_{g}
is the universal curve.

For the left-hand side, we consider the Leray spectral sequence for
p:\mathcal{C}_{g}\to\mathcal{H}_{g}
. It degenerates e.g. by
[
Del68
]
, and
Rp_{\ast}\mathbf{Q}\cong\mathbf{Q}\oplus\mathbb{H}[-1]\oplus\mathbf{Q}(-1)[-2].
Combining this with the projection formula, we see that

H^{k}(\mathcal{C}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\oplus H^{k-1}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H})\otimes\mathbb{H})\oplus H^{k-2}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))(-1).

We should explain why the middle term here leads to
-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}}
. Indeed, by Schur–Weyl duality the
z^{i/2}
-coefficient of
\Phi_{g}
is the class in
\widehat{\Lambda}
of the symmetric sequence
n\mapsto\mathrm{Gr}_{i}^{W}H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g},\mathbb{H}^{\otimes n})
. But then
[
GK98
, Eq. (7.5)]
the
z^{i/2}
-coefficient of
\frac{\partial\Phi_{g}}{\partial p_{1}}
is the class in
\widehat{\Lambda}
of the symmetric sequence
n\mapsto\mathrm{Gr}_{i}^{W}H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g},\mathbb{H}^{\otimes(n+1)})
.

For the right-hand side, we note that
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}
is a closed substack of
\mathcal{C}_{g}
, with open complement
\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1}
. The associated Gysin long exact sequence reads

\ldots\to H^{k-2}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))(-1)\to H^{k}(\mathcal{C}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\to H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))

\hskip 142.26378pt\to H^{k-1}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))(-1)\to\ldots

Taking Euler characteristics in this long exact sequence gives the result. Indeed, the terms of the form
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))(-1)
contribute exactly
z\Phi_{g,1,0}
to the right-hand side. (Recall that
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{V}
on
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}
.) We claim that the terms of the form
H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
contribute exactly
\sum_{j\geq 0}(-1)^{j}e_{j}\Phi_{g,0,1}
. Indeed, once we are taking Euler characteristics we may as well replace
\mathbb{H}
with the formal difference
\mathbb{V}-\mathbf{Q}
(considered in a suitable Grothendieck group), cf. §
10.0.1
, and formally one has

S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}-\mathbf{Q})=\bigoplus c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}S^{\mu}(\mathbb{V})\otimes S^{\nu}(-\mathbf{Q}),

where
c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
is a Littlewood–Richardson coefficient, cf. property (4) of §
2.2.13
. The term
S^{\nu}(-\mathbf{Q})
vanishes unless
\nu=(1^{j})
, i.e.
S^{\nu}
is an exterior power, in which case we have
\wedge^{j}(-\mathbf{Q})=(-1)^{j}\mathbf{Q}
. Since
e_{j}
is the symmetric function corresponding to
\wedge^{j}
the total contribution from these terms is
\sum_{j\geq 0}(-1)^{j}e_{j}\Phi_{g,0,1}
.
∎

10.0.6
.

Note that every symmetric function
f\in\Lambda
can be uniquely written as
f_{e}+f_{o}
with
f_{e}\in\bigoplus_{n}\Lambda_{2n}
and
f_{o}\in\bigoplus_{n}\Lambda_{2n+1}
. We call
f_{e}
and
f_{o}
the
even
and the
odd
part of
f
.

10.0.7 Theorem
.

The stable generating series for closed and open surfaces are related by

\Phi_{\infty}=\frac{z+\sum_{j\geq 0}e_{2j}}{1-z^{2}}\Phi_{\infty}^{0,1}.

Proof.

This identity is obtained from the preceding proposition by letting
g\to\infty
, and then taking the even part of the resulting expression.

Considering first the left-hand side of the preceding proposition, we note that the generating series
\Phi_{g}
is even for any
g
: the hyperelliptic involution implies that
S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H})
has no cohomology on
\mathcal{H}_{g}
when
|\lambda|
is odd. Hence on the left-hand side the even part of the expression is precisely
(1+z)\Phi_{g}
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi_{g}}{\partial p_{1}}
is purely odd.

On the right-hand side, we see similarly that
\Phi_{g,1,0}
is purely even for all
g
because of the hyperelliptic involution. The same argument does not apply to
\Phi_{g,0,1}
, but by homological stability we have
\Phi_{\infty,1,0}=\Phi_{\infty,0,1}
and a fortiori it is even as a symmetric function in the stable range. Hence taking the even part just amounts to discarding the terms
e_{j}
with odd
j
. Finally
\Phi_{\infty,0,1}=\frac{1}{1-z}\Phi_{\infty}^{0,1}
.
∎

10.0.8
.

We combine this formula with the plethystic formula for the stable Poincaré series of
4.4.12
, to obtain an analogue of
4.4.12
for closed surfaces.

10.0.9 Corollary
.

There is an identity

\sum_{k}\sum_{\lambda}\dim H_{k}(H_{\infty},V_{\lambda})(-z)^{k}s_{\lambda^{\prime}}=\frac{z+\sum_{j\geq 0}z^{j}h_{2j}}{1-z^{2}}\mathbf{Exp}(z^{-1}\mathbf{Log}(z+\sum_{r\geq 0}z^{r}h_{2r})-1-h_{2}).

Proof.

Indeed:
4.4.12
gives a formula for
\Phi_{\infty}^{0,1}
, and we merely need to plug it into
10.0.7
. What needs remarking is firstly that in
10.0.7
the term
S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H})
corresponds to the Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}
, and in
4.4.12
we find the transpose
s_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. To relate the formulas, we have to apply the involution
\omega
of the ring of symmetric functions to
10.0.7
, replacing
e_{j}
with
h_{j}
. Secondly,
10.0.7
is about generating series for weight-graded Euler characteristics, and
4.4.12
is a formula for Poincaré series. Since
H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
is pure of weight
2k+|\lambda|
, the two are related by a shift of
|\lambda|
, which results in replacing
h_{2j}
with
z^{j}h_{2j}
.
∎

10.0.10 Remark
.

It is quite curious that the same expression
z+\sum_{j\geq 0}z^{j}h_{2j}
appears twice in the formula for completely different reasons. We do not have a good conceptual explanation for why this should be the case.

10.0.11 Corollary
.

If
k\leq\tfrac{2g-1-3|\lambda|}{4}
then there are Galois-equivariant isomorphisms
H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g+1},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\cong H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g+2},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))\ldots

Proof.

Note first that since
H^{j}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))\cong H^{j}(\mathcal{H}_{g+1}^{1,0},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))
for
j\leq\tfrac{2g-1-|\lambda|}{2}
by the result of Randal-Williams–Wahl, it follows from the Deligne bounds on the weights of the unstable cohomology
[
Del80
]
that
\Phi_{g}^{1,0}
and
\Phi_{g+1}^{1,0}
have the same coefficient of
z^{i}s_{\lambda}
for all
i\leq\tfrac{2g-1+|\lambda|}{4}
. The arguments of this section show that the same holds for
\Phi_{g}
and
\Phi_{g+1}
.

We claim now that
\dim H^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
is the coefficient of
z^{k+|\lambda|/2}
in
\Phi_{g}
. Indeed,
10.0.3
shows in fact that
H^{i}(\mathcal{H}_{g},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{H}))
is pure Tate of weight
2i+|\lambda|
for all
i\leq 2k
, and by the Deligne bounds the weight
2k+|\lambda|
cannot occur above this degree.
∎

10.0.12 Remark
.

Armed with
10.0.9
instead of
4.4.12
one can give analogous proofs to show that

7.0.2
,
7.0.4
and
7.0.12
hold with
H_{\infty}^{0,1}

replaced by
H_{\infty}
. Similarly, a version
7.0.15
holds with

H_{\infty,1}
replaced by
H_{\infty}
, except one finds a polynomial of degree at most
|\lambda|
rather than exactly
|\lambda|-1
.

11.
Point counting

11.0.1
.

In this section we study the traces of Frobenius on the cohomology groups of the moduli space

\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}

with coefficients in the irreducible symplectic representations

V_{\lambda}.

In particular, we show that for any fixed finite field of odd characteristic and any
\lambda
of even weight, the limit as
g\to\infty
of the traces of Frobenius on these cohomology groups exists and is a rational function in the cardinality of the finite field. A similar result for the moduli space
\mathcal{H}_{g}
of closed hyperelliptic curves together with an algorithmic procedure to compute the rational functions occurring in the limit was obtained by the first named author
[
Ber09
, Section 13]
. However, rather than trying to compute these rational functions directly, it is more natural to study a certain generating function of the
stable
traces, to be introduced in Subsection
11.2
. As we shall see, this function is closely related
to the Poincaré series of the cohomologies
H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}),
and is precisely the so-called
arithmetic
factor occurring in the (conjectural) asymptotic formula for the moments of quadratic Dirichlet
L
-functions over rational function fields.

11.1.
Zeta functions and quadratic
L
-series

11.1.1
.

We begin by recalling some basic facts about zeta functions of algebraic curves over finite fields and quadratic
L
-series in the rational function field setting.

11.1.2
.

Let
\mathbf{F}_{q}
be a finite field with
q
elements, and let
\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q}
be a fixed algebraic closure of
\mathbf{F}_{q}.
Let
C
be a smooth, projective, and geometrically connected curve of genus
g
over
\mathbf{F}_{q}.
The zeta function of
C
is defined by

Z_{C}(t)=\exp\!\Bigg(\sum_{n\geq 1}\#C(\mathbf{F}_{q^{n}})\frac{t^{n}}{n}\Bigg)\qquad(\text{for $|t|<1/\penalty q$}).

11.1.3
.

For a prime
\ell
different from the characteristic of
\mathbf{F}_{q}
and
n\geq 1,
one can express the number of points as
\#C(\mathbf{F}_{q^{n}})=q^{n}+1-a_{n}(C),
where

a_{n}(C)=\Tr(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}^{n}\,|\,H_{\text{\'{e}t}}^{1}(\bar{C},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}))=\sum_{j=1}^{2g}\omega_{j}(C)^{n}.

Here

\bar{C}:=C\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{q}}\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q}

and
\mathrm{Frob}_{q}
is the endomorphism of the
\ell
-adic étale cohomology induced by the Frobenius morphism
\bar{C}\rightarrow\bar{C}.
Thus the zeta function is a rational function

Z_{C}(t)=\frac{P_{C}(t)}{(1-t)(1-qt)}

with numerator

P_{C}(t)\in\mathbf{Z}[t]

of degree
2g,
and constant term

P_{C}(0)=1.

Moreover, it satisfies the functional equation

Z_{C}(t)=\big(qt^{2}\big)^{g-1}Z_{C}(1/\penalty qt).

11.1.4
.

The zeta function satisfies the Riemann hypothesis
[
Wei48
]
, that is, the
normalized
eigenvalues of Frobenius

q^{-1/2}\omega_{j}(C)

(
j=1,\ldots,2g
) lie on the unit circle. Moreover, from the functional equation, these eigenvalues have the property that, after an eventual reordering,

\omega_{j}(C)\omega_{j+g}(C)=q

for all
1\leq j\leq g.
In other words, there exists a unique conjugacy class
\Theta(C/\penalty\mathbf{F}_{q})
in the similitude group
\mathrm{GSp}(2g)
such that

P_{C}(t)=\det(1-t\Theta(C/\penalty\mathbf{F}_{q})).

This conjugacy class is called the Frobenius conjugacy class attached to
C/\penalty\mathbf{F}_{q}.

11.1.5
.

Now assume that
\mathbf{F}_{q}
has odd characteristic.

11.1.6 Definition
.

For
d,p\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x],
with
p
irreducible, define the quadratic residue symbol
(\frac{d}{p})
to be
0
if
p\mid d,

1
if
d
is congruent to a square modulo
p
and
-1
otherwise. If
m
is non-constant, and

m=p_{1}\cdots\,p_{k}

with each
p_{i}\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x]
irreducible, one defines

(\frac{d}{m})=(\frac{d}{p_{1}})\cdots(\frac{d}{p_{k}})
.

11.1.7
.

It is clear that the quadratic residue symbol is completely multiplicative in both variables, that is,

{\displaystyle\left({\frac{d_{1}d_{2}}{m}}\right)}={\displaystyle\left({\frac{d_{1}}{m}}\right)}{\displaystyle\left({\frac{d_{2}}{m}}\right)}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;{\displaystyle\left({\frac{d}{m_{1}m_{2}}}\right)}={\displaystyle\left({\frac{d}{m_{1}}}\right)}{\displaystyle\left({\frac{d}{m_{2}}}\right)}

for all

d,d_{1},d_{2},m,m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x],

with

m,m_{1},m_{2}

non-constant. It is also easy to check that (see
[
Ros02
, Proposition 3.2]
)

{\displaystyle\left({\frac{c}{p}}\right)}=c^{\frac{q-1}{2}\deg p}

for
c\in\mathbf{F}_{q}
and
p\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x]
irreducible. Thus
(\frac{c}{p})=1
if

c\in(\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2},

and

(\frac{c}{p})=(-1)^{\deg p}

if

c\in\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}\setminus(\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}.

By induction on the irreducible factors,

(\frac{c}{m})=1

or
(-1)^{\deg m},
for all
m
of positive degree, according as
c
is a square in
\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}
or not.

11.1.8
.

The quadratic residue symbol also satisfies the reciprocity law
[
Ros02
, Theorem 3.5]
: for
d,m\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x]
monic and non-constant, we have

\begin{split}{\displaystyle\left({\frac{d}{m}}\right)}&=(-1)^{\frac{|d|-1}{2}\frac{|m|-1}{2}}{\displaystyle\left({\frac{m}{d}}\right)}\\
&=(-1)^{\frac{q-1}{2}\deg d\deg m}\,{\displaystyle\left({\frac{m}{d}}\right)}\end{split}

where for a non-zero element
a\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x],

|a|=q^{\deg a}
denotes its norm.

11.1.9
.

For
d\in\mathbf{F}_{q}[x]
square-free, define

\chi_{d}(m)=(\frac{d}{m}),

and extend it to all non-zero polynomials by setting

\chi_{d}(c)=1

for all
c\in\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}.
To
\chi_{d}
there is an associated
L
-function, first considered by E. Artin in his thesis
[
Art24
]
, defined for
s\in\mathbf{C}
with
\Re(s)>1
by

L(s,\chi_{d})\;\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\,\in\,\mathbf{F}_{q}[x]\\
m\text{ monic}\end{subarray}}\chi_{d}(m)|m|^{-s}=\,\prod_{p}(1-\chi_{d}(p)|p|^{-s})^{-1}

the product in the right-hand side being over all monic irreducibles. It is easy to see that indeed both the
sum and product converge absolutely when
\Re(s)>1.

11.1.10
.

If
d
is monic and square-free of degree
D\geq 3,
the
L
-function and the numerator

P_{C_{d}}(t)

of the zeta function of
C_{d}
defined by the affine model
y^{2}=d(x)
are connected by

L(s,\chi_{d})=(1-q^{-s})^{\delta_{D}}P_{C_{d}}(q^{-s}),\;\;\;\text{with $\delta_{D}=(1+(-1)^{D})/\penalty 2$.}

This follows by combining Proposition 14.9 and Proposition 17.7 in
[
Ros02
]
with the well-known fact that the zeta function of a smooth, projective, and geometrically connected algebraic curve over a finite field is the same as the zeta function of its function field. If
d
is monic of degree
D=1,2,
then

L(s,\chi_{d})=(1-q^{-s})^{\delta_{D}},

and for
d\in\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}
we have:

L(s,\chi_{d})=\zeta_{\mathbf{F}_{q}[x]}(s)=\frac{1}{1-q^{1-s}}\;\;\text{if $d\in(\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}$}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;L(s,\chi_{d})=\frac{1}{1+q^{1-s}}\;\;\text{if $d\notin(\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}$.}

Thus for
d
monic and square-free of degree
D>0,
the
L
-function is a polynomial in
q^{-s}
of degree
D-1,
and it satisfies the functional equation

(1-q^{-s})^{-\delta_{D}}L(s,\chi_{d})=q^{(\delta_{D}+1)(s-\frac{1}{2})}|d|^{\frac{1}{2}-s}(1-q^{s-1})^{-\delta_{D}}L(1-s,\chi_{d}).

11.1.11
.

In what follows, it will be often convenient to substitute

t:=q^{-s},
and denote the
L
-function by

\mathcal{L}(t,\chi_{d}).

11.2.
A generating function for stable traces

11.2.1
.

Let
\mathbf{F}_{q}
be a finite field of odd characteristic.
For
n>0,
let
\mathscr{P}_{n}
denote the set of monic square-free polynomials of degree
n
in
\mathbf{F}_{q}[x],
and for
d\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
, let
\Theta_{d}
denote the following multiset with
n-1
elements: if
n
is odd it is the eigenvalues occurring in the Frobenius conjugacy class of the hyperelliptic curve
C_{d}/\penalty\mathbf{F}_{q}
with affine equation
y^{2}=d(x)
; if
n
is even then
\Theta_{d}
contains in addition the number
1
. With this definition, we have

\mathcal{L}(t,\chi_{d})=\prod_{\omega\in\Theta_{d}}(1-\omega t)

for all
d
.

11.2.2
.

Define for
n>0
an element
\mathcal{Z}_{n}\in\widehat{\Lambda\otimes\mathbf{C}}
by the formula

\mathcal{Z}_{n}=q^{-n}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{n}}\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\Theta_{d})\cdot s_{\lambda^{\prime}},

where
s_{\lambda}(\Theta_{d})
denotes the Schur polynomial in
n-1
variables evaluated on the elements of
\Theta_{d}
.
The goal of this subsection is to study the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n}
. In particular, the limit exists in the sense that its coefficients (say with respect to the basis of Schur polynomials) converge pointwise. We call this limit the generating function of stable traces. It may be written explicitly as follows:

11.2.3 Theorem
.

The generating function of the stable traces is given by

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n}=\mathbf{Exp}\Big(q\,\mathbf{Log}\Big(q^{-1}+\sum_{k\geq 0}h_{2k}\Big)-1\Big)

where
\mathbf{Exp}
and
\mathbf{Log}
are the plethystic exponential and logarithm, respectively.

11.2.4
.

The generating series
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
admits a cohomological interpretation, given in
11.2.6
. Via this interpretation,
11.2.3
follows from
1.1.6
and
1.1.10
, together with the bound on the unstable Betti numbers of the braid groups given in
11.3.13
. However, in this subsection we give a direct proof of
11.2.3
by analytic methods, with an explicit error term. While not logically necessary, since it follows from our topological results, we believe that the argument is enlightening and clarifies the difference between
11.2.3
and
1.1.16
/
1.1.14
.

11.2.5 Remark
.

11.2.3
will be deduced from an asymptotic formula proven in
11.2.11
. The latter formula is in a sense not new, see, for instance,
[
Rud10
,
RG12
]
and
[
BJ19
]
, where the authors obtained similar asymptotics, but for averages of products of power sum polynomials in the eigenvalues of Frobenius rather than products of elementary symmetric polynomials. However, any of these asymptotic formulas (especially that in
[
RG12
]
, which is closely related to ours) is insufficient for our purposes, mainly due to the restriction to partitions whose parts grow at least logarithmically in the genus
g.

11.2.6 Lemma
.

We have the equality

\mathcal{Z}_{n}=\begin{cases}\sum_{\lambda}\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}|H_{k}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))\cdot s_{\lambda^{\prime}}&\text{if }n=2g+1,\\
\sum_{\lambda}\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}|H_{k}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))\cdot s_{\lambda^{\prime}}&\text{if }n=2g+2.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let us say that
n=2g+1
is odd (the argument in the even case is identical).
By the Grothendieck-Lefschetz trace formula for stacks
[
Beh03
, Corollary 6.4.10]
one has

\sum_{k}\,(-1)^{k}\Tr(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\,|\,H_{c,\text{\'{e}t}}^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{q}}\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},S^{\lambda}(\mathbb{V}))\,=\,\sum_{[C_{d}]\in\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}(\mathbf{F}_{q})}s_{\lambda}(\Theta_{d}).

The left-hand side equals
q^{\dim\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}}=q^{2g}
times the trace of Frobenius on étale homology, by Poincaré duality. As for the right-hand side, observe that
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}\cong\mathrm{Conf}_{2g+1}(\mathbb{A}^{1})/\mathbb{G}_{a}
, meaning that
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}(\mathbf{F}_{q})
equals
\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}
up to the action by translation, which accounts for the additional factor of
q
. Finally, the multiset
\Theta_{d}
consists precisely of the eigenvalues of
\mathrm{Frob}_{q}
on the stalk of
\mathbb{V}
at a geometric point above
[C_{d}]\in\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}(\mathbf{F}_{q})
. ∎

11.2.7
.

From the definition of
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
we see that if we specialize
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
to a symmetric function of finitely many variables
\mathrm{t}=t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{r}
, then

\mathcal{Z}_{n}(\mathrm{t})=q^{-n}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\,\mathcal{L}(-t_{i},\chi_{d}).

Indeed, by the dual Cauchy identity (§
2.2.8
) we have for any
d\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
that

\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\Theta_{d})s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})=\prod_{\omega\in\Theta_{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1+\omega t_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{L}(-t_{i},\chi_{d}).

11.2.8
.

Now define

\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{t},\xi)\,=\,\sum_{n}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(\mathrm{t})\,\xi^{n}\,=\sum_{d\text{ monic \& sq. free}}\,\,\prod_{i=1}^{r}\,\mathcal{L}(-t_{i},\chi_{d})\,(\xi/q)^{\deg d}.

Write
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{t},\xi)=\sum_{\lambda}\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)\mathrm{t}^{\lambda}
, where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
is a tuple of nonnegative integers.

11.2.9 Remark
.

If
n
is odd and
|\lambda|
is odd, then the coefficient of
\xi^{n}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\mathrm{t})
vanishes. Indeed, this follows by a point-counting argument: if

c\in\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}\setminus(\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times})^{2}
, then

\mathcal{L}(t,\chi_{cd})=\mathcal{L}(-t,\chi_{d})
, and by pairing up the terms corresponding to
d(x)
and
c^{-n}d(cx)
we see that the sum vanishes. It can also be seen via
4.3.6
and the cohomological interpretation of
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
.

11.2.10 Lemma
.

Suppose
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
. Then

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(q\xi)=(-1)^{|\lambda|}(1-q\xi^{2})\sum\frac{\mathcal{L}(\xi,\chi_{u})}{\prod_{p\mid u}(1-\xi^{2\deg p})\prod_{p\mid v}(1+\chi_{u}(p)\xi^{\deg p})}

where the sum is over all monic polynomials

m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}

with

\deg m_{i}=\lambda_{i},

and for each choice of
m_{i}
,

1\leq i\leq r
, we write

m_{1}\cdots\,m_{r}=uv^{2}

with
u,v
monic and
u

square-free.

Proof.

This is merely an application of the quadratic reciprocity law

{\displaystyle\left({\frac{d}{m}}\right)}=(-1)^{\frac{q-1}{2}\deg d\deg m}\,{\displaystyle\left({\frac{m}{d}}\right)}.

To see this, assume
|t_{i}|<1/\penalty q

for
i=1,\ldots,r,
and
|\xi|<1/\penalty q.
Then, by expressing each

\mathcal{L}(-t_{i},\chi_{d})

by its defining series, interchanging the order of summation and applying
quadratic reciprocity, we can write

\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{t},q\xi)\;\;=\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,\,m_{r}\text{ monic}}\;\prod_{i=1}^{r}(-t_{i})^{\deg m_{i}}\sum_{d\text{ monic \& sq.\ free}}\,{\displaystyle\left({\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}}{d}}\right)}\xi^{\deg d}.

Here we used that the sign in the quadratic reciprocity can always be taken to be
1
: if not, then
\deg d
and
\deg m=|\lambda|
are both odd, but in this case the corresponding coefficient of
\mathcal{Z}(t,\xi)
vanishes.
For fixed

m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}
,
write

m_{1}\cdots\,m_{r}=uv^{2}

with
u,v
monic and
u
square-free. Then we can express
the inner sum as

\sum_{d\text{ monic \& sq.\ free}}\,{\displaystyle\left({\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}}{d}}\right)}\xi^{\deg d}=\sum_{d\text{ monic \& sq.\ free}}\chi_{uv^{2}}(d)\,\xi^{\deg d}=\prod_{p\,\nmid\,uv}\big(1+\chi_{u}(p)\,\xi^{\deg p}\big)

the product being over all monic irreducibles not dividing

uv
.
For each such
p
, we write

1+\chi_{u}(p)\,\xi^{\deg p}=\frac{1-\xi^{2\deg p}}{1-\chi_{u}(p)\xi^{\deg p}},

and thus

\sum_{d\text{ monic \& sq.\ free}}\,{\displaystyle\left({\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}}{d}}\right)}\xi^{\deg d}=\big(1-q\xi^{2}\big)\frac{\mathcal{L}(\xi,\chi_{u})}{\prod_{p\mid u}\big(1-\xi^{2\deg p}\big)\prod_{p\mid v}\big(1+\chi_{u}(p)\xi^{\deg p}\big)}.

Here we used that
\prod_{p}(1-\xi^{2\deg p})=(1-q\xi^{2})
. The result follows.
∎

11.2.11 Proposition
.

For
n>0
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})

the coefficient of
\xi^{n}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)
is given by

\big(1-q^{-1}\big)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\cdots\,m_{r}=v^{2}\\
\deg m_{i}=\lambda_{i}\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{p\mid v}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{|p|}\bigg)^{-1}\;+\;\;O\Big(5^{|\lambda|}q^{|\lambda|-n/\penalty 2}\Big).

Note in particular that the sum is empty if
|\lambda|
is odd.

Proof.

First notice that the coefficient
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)
of
\mathrm{t}^{\lambda}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{t},\xi)
(see
11.2.10
) is a holomorphic function in the open disc
|\xi|<q,
except for the factor
\mathcal{L}(\xi/q,\chi_{u})
which picks up a simple pole at
\xi=1
when
u=1
. Thus, the coefficient of
\xi^{n}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)
is given by

\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\;\oint\limits_{|\xi|\,=\,\varepsilon}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)}{\xi^{n+1}}\,d\xi

where
0<\varepsilon<1
. Consider the integral

\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{\partial\mathscr{A}}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)}{\xi^{n+1}}\,d\xi

where

\mathscr{A}=\{\xi\in\mathbf{C}:\varepsilon\leq|\xi|\leq q^{1/\penalty 2}\}.

Note that

(3)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\;\,\oint\limits_{|\xi|\,=\,q^{1/\penalty 2}}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)}{\xi^{n+1}}\,d\xi\,\right|\leq q^{-n/2}\cdot\sup_{|\xi|\,=\,q^{1/\penalty 2}}|\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)|.

We now bound
\sup_{|\xi|\,=\,q^{1/\penalty 2}}|\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)|=\sup_{|\xi|\,=\,q^{-1/\penalty 2}}|\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(q\xi)|
using
11.2.10
. When
|\xi|=q^{\,-1/\penalty 2}
we have the following simple estimates:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}(\xi,\chi_{d})|

\displaystyle=\prod_{\omega\in\Theta_{d}}\big|1-\omega\xi\big|\leq 2^{\deg d-1},

\displaystyle\prod_{p\mid u}|1-\xi^{2\deg p}|\prod_{p\mid v}|1+\chi_{u}(p)\xi^{\deg p}|

\displaystyle\geq(1-q^{-1/2})^{\deg u+\deg v}\geq(1-q^{-1/2})^{|\lambda|}.

Thus, for
|\xi|=q^{\,-1/\penalty 2},
we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(q\xi)|&\leq 2\sum\sup_{|\xi|\,=\,q^{\,-1/\penalty 2}}\bigg(\frac{|\mathcal{L}(\xi,\chi_{u})|}{\prod_{p\mid u}\big|1-\xi^{2\deg p}\big|\prod_{p\mid v}\big|1+\chi_{u}(p)\xi^{\deg p}\big|}\bigg)\\
&\leq\bigg(\frac{2}{1-q^{-1/2}}\bigg)^{|\lambda|}\cdot\,\#\{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}):\text{$\deg m_{i}=\lambda_{i}$
for $i=1,\ldots,r$}\}\\
&\leq 5^{|\lambda|}q^{|\lambda|}.\end{split}

Accordingly the integral (
3
) is

\leq 5^{|\lambda|}q^{|\lambda|-n/\penalty 2}.
On the other hand, by the residue theorem we have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{\partial\mathscr{A}}\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)}{\xi^{n+1}}\,d\xi&=\operatorname{Res}_{\xi=1}\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\xi)\\
&=-\,\bigg(1-\frac{1}{q}\bigg)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\cdots\,m_{r}=v^{2}\\
\deg m_{i}=\lambda_{i}\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{p\mid v}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{|p|}\bigg)^{-1}\end{split}

and the asymptotic formula follows. This completes the proof.
∎

11.2.12
.

We may now prove
11.2.3
.

Proof of
11.2.3
.

Let
\mu(n)
be the usual Möbius function, and let

i_{n}(q)=n^{-1}\sum_{l\mid n}\mu(n/\penalty l)q^{l}

be the number of monic irreducibles of degree
n
in
\mathbf{F}_{q}[x].

Then we have by
11.2.11

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n}(\mathrm{t})\;\,&=(1-q^{-1})\sum_{m_{1}\cdots\,m_{r}=v^{2}}\;\prod_{p\mid v}\big(1+|p|^{-1}\big)^{-1}\cdot\,t_{1}^{\deg m_{1}}\cdots\,t_{r}^{\deg m_{r}}\\
&=(1-q^{-1})\prod_{p}\Bigg(1\,+\,\big(1+|p|^{-1}\big)^{-1}\cdot\sum_{k>0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,\,k_{r}\geq 0\\
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}=2k\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}t_{j}^{k_{j}\deg p}\Bigg)\\
&=(1-q^{-1})\prod_{n>0}\Big(1\,+\,\big(1+q^{-n}\big)^{-1}\sum_{k>0}h_{2k}(\mathrm{t}^{n})\Big)^{i_{n}(q)}\end{split}

where

\mathrm{t}^{n}:=(t_{1}^{n},\ldots,t_{r}^{n}).

The result follows by comparing this expression with
7.0.14
.
∎

11.3.
Asymptotics for moments from homological stability

11.3.1
.

We will prefer to think about the representation theory of the usual symplectic groups, rather than the odd symplectic groups. For this reason, we work consistently in this section with the moduli spaces
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{g,1,0}
rather than
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{0,1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{g,0,1}
. We will also find it convenient to consider the
unitarized
conjugacy class of Frobenius. For
d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}
we write
\overline{\Theta}_{d}
for
q^{-1/2}\Theta_{d}
, which we may think of as a conjugacy class in
\operatorname{USp}(2g)
. Then
\overline{\Theta}_{d}
consists of the eigenvalues of Frobenius on the stalk above the point
[C_{d}]
of
\mathbb{V}(\tfrac{1}{2})
, the half-integral Tate twist of the standard local system.

11.3.2 Definition
.

Introduce the notation

\mathrm{tr}_{\lambda}(g)=\frac{1}{q^{2g+1}}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}s_{\langle\lambda\rangle}(\overline{\Theta}_{d}),

where
s_{\langle\lambda\rangle}
denotes a symplectic Schur function. We also set

T_{\lambda}=\lim_{g\to\infty}\mathrm{tr}_{\lambda}(g).

11.3.3
.

Applying the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula as in
11.2.6
we have

\mathrm{tr}_{\lambda}(g)=\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\mid H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{|\lambda|}{2})))

and

T_{\lambda}=\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\mid H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}_{q},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{|\lambda|}{2}))).

11.3.4
.

In the previous subsection we used crucially that

\mathcal{Z}_{n}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})=q^{-n}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{L}(-t_{i},\chi_{d});

this was a consequence of the dual Cauchy identity. As explained in §
2.6.8
there is a perfect symplectic analogue of the dual Cauchy identity when one considers symmetric functions in
infinitely
many variables, but it does not specialize in a useful way to finitely many variables. As an adequate substitute we will use an identity due to Jimbo–Miwa, see

[
JM85
]
,
[
How95
, Theorem 3.8.9.3]
, and
[
BG06
, Lemma 4]
.

11.3.5 Definition
.

Let
\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})
be a partition, i.e.
\lambda_{1}\leq r
and
\ell(\lambda)\leq g
. We set

\lambda^{{\dagger}}:=(g-\lambda_{r}^{\prime}\geq\cdots\geq g-\lambda_{1}^{\prime}),

where the non-zero integers among
\lambda_{j}^{\prime},

1\leq j\leq r,
are the parts of the conjugate partition
\lambda^{\prime}
of
\lambda.
If we want to make the dependence of
\lambda^{\dagger}
on
g
explicit, we write
\lambda^{\dagger}(g)
.

11.3.6 Theorem

(Jimbo–Miwa)
.

\prod_{i=1}^{g}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(x_{i}+x_{i}^{-1}+t_{j}+t_{j}^{-1})=\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}s_{\langle\lambda\rangle}(x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{g}^{\pm})s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm}).

11.3.7 Corollary
.

For complex variables

t_{1},\ldots,t_{r},

we have

\frac{1}{q^{2g+1}}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathcal{L}(q^{-1/2}t_{j},\chi_{d})\,=\,(t_{1}\cdots\,t_{r})^{g}\!\!\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\mathrm{tr}_{\lambda}(g)s_{\langle\lambda^{{\dagger}}\rangle}\big(t_{1}^{\pm 1}\!,\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm 1}\big).

Proof.

First rewrite
11.3.6
as

\prod_{i=1}^{g}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(1+t_{j}x_{i})(1+t_{j}x_{i}^{-1})=(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{g}\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}s_{\langle\lambda\rangle}(x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{g}^{\pm})s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm}).

Then set
\{x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{g}^{\pm}\}
equal to
\overline{\Theta}_{d}
, and observe that

\mathcal{L}(-q^{-1/2}t_{j},\chi_{d})=\prod_{\omega\in\overline{\Theta}_{d}}(1+t_{j}\omega)
. Finally, take the product over
r
and sum over
d
, and observe that the result is invariant under
t_{j}\mapsto-t_{j}
since
\mathrm{tr}_{\lambda}(g)
vanishes for
|\lambda|
odd.
∎

11.3.8 Corollary
.

\frac{1}{q^{2g+1}}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}{L}(\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d})^{r}\,=\,\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\mathrm{tr}_{\lambda}(g)\,\dim V_{\lambda^{\dagger}},

where
V_{\lambda^{\dagger}}
denotes an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{Sp}(2r)
.

Proof.

Set
t_{1}=t_{2}=\ldots=1
in the preceding corollary.
∎

11.3.9
.

We will in particular make use of the identity

\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\dim V_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{Sp}(2g)}\dim V_{\lambda^{\dagger}}^{\mathrm{Sp}(2r)}=4^{gr}

obtained by setting all
x_{i}
and
t_{i}
equal to
1
in
11.3.6
. Here the superscripts
\mathrm{Sp}(2g)
, resp.
\mathrm{Sp}(2r)
, indicate which groups
V_{\lambda}
, resp.
V_{\lambda^{\dagger}}
, are representations of.

11.3.10
.

If
\ell(\lambda)>g
then we can still define
\lambda^{\dagger}
as in
11.3.5
, but
\lambda^{\dagger}
is no longer dominant. We extend the definition of
s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}
to this case by plugging
\lambda^{\dagger}
into the Weyl character formula. Let

\alpha:=\{\lambda^{\dagger}+\rho\}-\rho,

where
\{\cdot\}
denotes the unique dominant weight conjugate to a given weight and
\rho
is the sum of the fundamental weights. Then
s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}
vanishes if
\alpha
is not dominant, and
s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}
is
\pm
the character of
V_{\alpha}
if
\alpha
is dominant.

11.3.11 Definition
.

Let

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r};g,q)=(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{g}\sum_{\lambda_{1}\leq r}T_{\lambda}\,s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}(g)\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm}).

We will see shortly that the sum converges.

11.3.12
.

One can show using the Weyl dimension formula that

\dim V_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{Sp}(2r)}=\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(\mu_{i}^{2}-\mu_{j}^{2})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}}{(2r-1)!!}

where
\mu_{i}=\lambda_{i}+r-i+1
. It is straightforward to see from this expression that for each
\lambda
with
\lambda_{1}\leq r
, the function

g\mapsto\dim V_{\lambda^{\dagger}(g)}

is a polynomial in
g
of degree
r(r+1)/2
. In particular,
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(1,\ldots,1;g,q)
is a polynomial in
g
of degree
r(r+1)/2
, for any fixed
q
.

11.3.13 Lemma
.

Let
V
be a representation of the braid group
\beta_{n}
. Then
\dim H_{k}(\beta_{n},V)\leq\binom{n-1}{k}\dim(V)
.

Proof.

By Poincaré duality, it suffices to bound the dimension of
H^{2n-k}_{c}(\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbf{C}),V)
.
Use the cellular chain complex associated to the Fuks stratification of
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(\mathbf{C})
(see
[
FN62
,
Fuk70
]
and
[
ETW17
, Theorem 4.3]
). It has exactly
\binom{n-1}{k}
cells of dimension
2n-k
.
∎

11.3.14 Lemma
.

Let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
be on the unit circle, and consider a partition
\lambda
with
\lambda_{1}\leq r
. Then
|s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}(g)\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})|\leq 4^{rg}\dim S^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{2r})
.

Proof.

We have
\lambda^{\dagger}=(g-\lambda^{\prime}_{r},\ldots,g-\lambda_{1}^{\prime})
. Let
\alpha
be as in §
11.3.10
. Then we can write
\alpha=\beta+\gamma
with
\beta\subseteq(g^{r})
and
\gamma\subseteq\lambda^{\prime}
. Thus,

|s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}(g)\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})|\leq\dim V_{\alpha}\leq\dim V_{\beta}\dim V_{\gamma}\leq\dim V_{(g^{r})}\dim V_{\lambda^{\prime}}\leq 4^{gr}\dim S^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{2r})

using in the last step §
11.3.9
for the first factor, and that
V_{\lambda^{\prime}}
is a summand of
S^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{2r})
for the second factor.
∎

11.3.15 Lemma
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
N\in\mathbf{N}
there is a bound

\sum_{|\lambda|=w}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})\dim S^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{N})\ll_{N,\varepsilon}2^{\varepsilon k}.

Proof.

Consider the generating function of stable Poincaré series

\mathbf{Exp}\Big(\,z^{-1}\,\mathbf{Log}\Big(z+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{k}h_{2k}(\mathrm{t})\Big)-h_{2}(\mathrm{t})-1\Big).

According to
4.4.12
, the left-hand side of
11.3.15
is the coefficient of
z^{k}t^{w}
of the function obtained by setting
t_{1}=\ldots=t_{N}=t
,
t_{i}=0
for
i>N
, in this generating series. By
12.2.4
and the comparison explained in §
12.2.2
the series converges to a holomorphic function on the region
7
7

7

For our purposes it would be enough to know convergence in the region
|z|<1
,
|tz|<1
, which is simpler to show than
12.2.4
.

|z|<1
,
|t^{4}z|<1
. Now apply Cauchy’s inequality, i.e. extract the coefficient of
z^{k}t^{w}
using Cauchy’s integral formula applied to the circles
|z|=2^{-\varepsilon}
,
|t|=1
. ∎

11.3.16 Definition
.

Let
\theta:\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{R}
be a monotone increasing function. We say that
\theta
is a
uniform stability bound
if for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
,

H_{k}(\beta_{n},V_{\lambda})\to H_{k}(\beta_{n+1},V_{\lambda})

is an isomorphism for all
k\leq\theta(n)
, for all
\lambda
.

11.3.17
.

Let
\theta
be a uniform stability bound. Then
H_{k}(\beta_{\infty},V_{\lambda})\cong H_{k}(\beta_{n},V_{\lambda})

for all
k\leq\theta(n)
. But observe that
V_{\lambda}
is zero as a representation of
\beta_{n}
if
\tfrac{n}{2}<\ell(\lambda)
. Thus,
H_{k}(\beta_{\infty},V_{\lambda})=0
for
k<\theta(2\ell(\lambda))
. In
7.0.12
we proved that
H_{k}(\beta_{\infty},V_{\lambda})=0
for
k<\ell(\lambda)/2
, and this bound is sharp. Thus, without loss of generality a uniform stability bound must satisfy
\theta(n)\leq\lfloor\tfrac{n}{4}\rfloor
. Our conjecture is that this bound is close to sharp.

11.3.18 Conjecture
.

There exists a uniform stability bound
\theta(n)=\tfrac{1}{4}n-c
, where
c\geq 0
is an absolute constant.

11.3.19 Theorem
.

Suppose that
\theta
is a uniform stability bound. Then for any
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
on the unit circle

\frac{1}{q^{2g+1}}\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{L}(q^{-1/2}t_{i},\chi_{d})=\mathcal{Q}_{1}(t;g,q)+O_{\theta}\Big(4^{g(r+1)}q^{-\theta(2g+1)/2}\Big),

the implied constant depending upon
\theta
in the
O
-symbol being explicitly computable.

Proof.

The left-hand side of
11.3.19
equals

(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{g}\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\mid H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{|\lambda|}{2})))s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})

and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(t,g)
equals

(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{g}\sum_{\lambda_{1}\leq r}\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\mid H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{|\lambda|}{2})))s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm}).

Subtracting the two, all terms with
k\leq\theta(2g+1)
cancel by homological stability. To estimate the difference, we first consider the contribution from the unstable homology. Using the Deligne bounds on Frobenius eigenvalues from
[
Del80
]
,
11.3.13
, and the identity of §
11.3.9
, we get

\displaystyle\left|(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{g}\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\mid H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{|\lambda|}{2})))s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}q^{-k/2}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})\dim V_{\lambda^{\dagger}}^{\mathrm{Sp}(2r)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}q^{-k/2}\binom{2g}{k}}_{\ll\,2^{2g}\,q^{-\theta(2g+1)/2}}\underbrace{\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\dim V_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{Sp}(2g)}\dim V_{\lambda^{\dagger}}^{\mathrm{Sp}(2r)}}_{=\,\,4^{gr}}\ll 4^{g(r+1)}q^{-\theta(2g+1)/2}.

We now have to estimate the contribution from the stable homology, i.e. to bound the quantity

(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{g}\sum(-1)^{k}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q}\mid H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{|\lambda|}{2})))s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})

where we sum over
k
and
\lambda
such that
\lambda_{1}\leq r
and
either

\ell(\lambda)>g
or
k>\theta(2g+1)
(or both).

We split this sum into two: first when
|\lambda|\leq 2g
, then when
|\lambda|>2g
. Recall that the stable homology is pure of weight
-2k
in degree
k
. We claim that the first sum can be bounded by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|\leq 2g\\
\lambda_{1}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}q^{-k}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(1^{\pm},\ldots,1^{\pm}).

Indeed, if
\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})
it is clear that we can start the summation from
k>\theta(2g+1)
. When
\ell(\lambda)>g
we instead observe that the stable homology vanishes below degree
g/2
by
7.0.12
, which is at least
\theta(2g+1)
by §
11.3.17
. Now using
11.3.14
and
11.3.15
we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|\leq 2g\\
\lambda_{1}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}q^{-k}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(1^{\pm},\ldots,1^{\pm})

\displaystyle\leq 4^{gr}

\displaystyle\sum_{w=0}^{2g}\sum_{|\lambda|=w}\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}q^{-k}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})\dim S^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{2r})

\displaystyle\ll 4^{gr}

\displaystyle\sum_{w=0}^{2g}\sum_{k>\theta(2g+1)}q^{-k}2^{\varepsilon k}\ll 4^{gr}(2g+1)2^{\varepsilon\theta(2g+1)}q^{-\theta(2g+1)}

for all
\varepsilon>0
. The second sum is treated in the exact same way, but we use instead that
H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})=0
for
k<|\lambda|/4
(
7.0.2
). We obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|>2g\\
\lambda_{1}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sum_{k\geq|\lambda|/4}q^{-k}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}\rangle}(1^{\pm},\ldots,1^{\pm})

\displaystyle\leq 4^{gr}

\displaystyle\sum_{w>2g}\sum_{|\lambda|=w}\sum_{k\geq w/4}q^{-k}\dim H_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{1,0},\mathbb{V}_{\lambda})\dim S^{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{2r})

\displaystyle\ll 4^{gr}

\displaystyle\sum_{w>2g}\sum_{k\geq w/4}q^{-k}2^{\varepsilon k}\ll 4^{gr}2^{\varepsilon g/2}q^{-g/2}.\qed

11.3.20 Remark
.

As stated in
11.3.18
, we expect
\theta(n)\approx n/4
, which leads to an error term in
11.3.19
which is roughly
O(q^{-g/4})
. On the other hand, as we explain in the next section of the paper, one expects an asymptotic formula with successive terms
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, in which the first term
\mathcal{T}_{1}
accounts for the contribution from stable homology. One also expects the error term in the asymptotic formula of
11.3.19
to be comparable to the second term
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, which is approximately
O(q^{-g/2})
. Looking through the proof of
11.3.19
, the discrepancy comes from the contributions from the homology of
\beta_{n}
which is
just outside
the stable range. In the stable range we know that Frobenius eigenvalues on
H_{k}
are
q^{-k}
, but outside the stable range we only have the Deligne bounds, giving the upper bound
q^{-k/2}
. Our expectation is that in the regime just outside the stable range we have
secondary stability

[
GKRW19
]
, and that just as the stable homology is pure of lowest possible weight, also the homology in the range where secondary stability holds has weights (close to) the lowest possible. This would account for the mismatch of error terms in
11.3.19
and the next section.

11.3.21 Remark
.

It seems likely that a version of
11.3.19
holds also when summing over polynomials of even degree; indeed, our homological stability results are valid for braid groups with both an odd or even number of strands. Pursuing this will require a more detailed study of the representation theory of odd symplectic groups than we want to include here.

12.
Moments and stability predictions

12.0.1
.

We now turn our attention to the important problem of determining the asymptotics of the traces
\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)
as the genus
g
grows, for all partitions
\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})
(i.e., all partitions whose Young diagram fit inside a rectangle of
g
rows and
r
columns), where
r
is a fixed positive integer. Since
\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)=0
when
|\lambda|
is odd, we can consider only the partitions of even weight. When
|\lambda|\leq 2g-1,
an asymptotic formula for
\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)
can be obtained by combining Proposition
11.2.11
with the results of Rudnick
[
Rud10
, Theorem 1]
and Roditty-Gershon
[
RG12
, Theorem 1.1]
. Obtaining a similar result in a wider range of
\lambda
seems to be a challenging problem. In fact,
to the best of our knowledge, at present, there is not even a prediction as to what the asymptotic behavior of these traces of Frobenius should be, when
|\lambda|
is quite large compared with
g.
The only existing result for general partitions comes from the seminal work of Katz and Sarnak, see
[
KS99
, Theorem 10.8.2]
:

12.0.2 Theorem

(Katz–Sarnak)
.

There exist positive constants
A(g)
and
C(g)
such that

|\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)|\leq C(g)(\dim\,V_{\lambda})q^{-1/\penalty 2}

for all
\lambda
, as long as

q=\#\mathbf{F}_{q}\geq A(g).

Here
V_{\lambda}
is the irreducible representation of
\mathrm{USp}(2g)
with highest weight
\lambda.

12.1.
The general conjecture

12.1.1
.

To set up some notation, let
A=(A_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq r+1}
be the symmetric matrix whose entries are

A_{ij}=\begin{cases}2&\mbox{if $i=j$}\\
-1&\mbox{if $i\neq j$ and either $i=r+1$ or $j=r+1$}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

To
A
one can attach a Kac-Moody Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}(A)
with the set
of roots
\Delta,
and

\{\alpha_{i}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq r+1}\subset\Delta

a system of simple roots; the reader interested in the general theory of Kac-Moody algebras can consult
[
Kac90
]
. We have that
\det A=-\,2^{r-1}(r-4).
By
[
Kac90
, Theorem 4.3]
, one finds at once that
A
is of finite, affine, or indefinite type when
r\leq 3,

r=4,

or
r\geq 5,
respectively. For example, the Dynkin diagram of
A
is
D_{4}

when
r=3
and
\tilde{D}_{4}
when
r=4;
for
r\geq 5,
the Dynkin diagram has a star-like shape. Let
W_{r}
denote the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}(A),
and let
\Delta^{\mathrm{re}}_{+}\subset\Delta
be the set of positive real roots. For

\alpha\in\Delta^{\mathrm{re}}_{+},\alpha=\sum_{i}k_{i}\alpha_{i}

(
k_{i}\in\mathbf{N}
), let

d(\alpha)=\sum_{i}k_{i}
denote the height of
\alpha,
and for each positive integer
n,
let
\Phi_{n}
be the subset of
\Delta^{\mathrm{re}}_{+}
with
k_{r+1}=n;

by
[
DT23
, Lemma 2.2]
,
\Phi_{n}
is a finite set. Finally, for each
\alpha\in\Phi_{n},
we fix a Weyl group element
w_{\alpha}
in reduced form of length

l(w_{\alpha})
such that

w_{\alpha}(\alpha)=\alpha_{r+1}.

This choice is possible since all real roots are in the orbit

W_{r}\alpha_{r+1},

see
[
DT23
, Lemma 2.3]
.

12.1.2
.

Now consider the
r
th moment-sums of quadratic
L
-functions, that is, the coefficients of the moment generating function

\mathcal{Z}(\mathrm{t},\xi)
. Initially, we take the variables to be

t_{i}=q^{-s_{i}},

for
i=1,\ldots,r.
In
[
DT23
]
, it has been conjectured that, for

\Re(s_{i})=\frac{1}{2}

and
N\geq 1
an integer, we have

(4)

\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}L(s_{i},\chi_{d})\,=\sum_{n\leq N}Q_{n,r}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime};2g+1,q)\,+\,O_{\Theta,\,q,\,r}\left(q^{(2g+1)(1+\Theta)/\penalty 2}\right)

for any

(N+1)^{-1}<\Theta<N^{-1}.

12.1.3
.

The terms

Q_{n,r}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime};2g+1,q),

\mathrm{s}^{\prime}:=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}),

have a somewhat complicated expression of the form

Q_{n,r}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime};2g+1,q)=n^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi_{n}}\bigg\{\sum_{a\in\{1,\,\theta_{0}\}}\;\sum_{\zeta_{a}^{n}=\mathrm{sgn}(a)}\frac{\Gamma_{w_{\alpha}}(a;\zeta_{a})}{2^{l(w_{\alpha})}}S_{\alpha}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime},\zeta_{a})\zeta_{a}^{2g+1}\bigg\}\,q^{\frac{(2g+1)(d(\alpha)+1-2\alpha(\mathrm{s}^{\prime}))}{2n}}

where
\theta_{0}
is any fixed non-square in
\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times},
and
\mathrm{sgn}(a)=1
or
-1
according as

a=1
or
\theta_{0}.
In addition, for

\alpha=\!\sum k_{i}\alpha_{i}\in\Delta^{\mathrm{re}}_{+},

we set

\alpha(\mathrm{s}^{\prime}):=\sum_{i\leq r}k_{i}s_{i}.

The function

S_{\alpha}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime},\zeta_{a})

decomposes as

S_{\alpha}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime},\zeta_{a})=\prod_{p}S_{p}^{w_{\alpha}}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime},\zeta_{a})

over all monic irreducibles, with

S_{p}^{w_{\alpha}}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime},\zeta_{a})

given in
[
DT23
, Sec. ​5.2]
. An explicit formula for

2^{-l(w_{\alpha})}\Gamma_{\!w_{\alpha}}\!(a;\zeta_{a})

can be found in
[
DT23
, Sec. ​5.1]
. When
r=1,2,
the sets
\Phi_{n}

(
n\geq 2
) are all empty, and when
r=3
the same is true, but for
n\geq 3.

If
r\geq 4,
then
\Phi_{n}
is non-empty for all
n\geq 1.
Consequently,
Q_{n,1}=Q_{n,2}=0
for
n\geq 2,
and
Q_{n,3}=0
for
n\geq 3.
For our purposes here, we shall only need the first two terms of the asymptotic formula of (
4
), when
r\geq 3
.

12.1.4
.

Prior to
[
DT23
]
, Andrade and Keating
[
AK14
, Conjecture 5]
made the weaker conjecture that

\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}L(\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d})^{r}=\sum_{d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}}Q_{r}(\log_{q}|d|)(1+o(1))

where
Q_{r}
is an explicit polynomial of degree
r(r+1)/\penalty 2.
Note that
Q_{r}(\log_{q}|d|)=Q_{r}(2g+1)
for all

d\in\mathscr{P}_{2g+1},
and thus the right-hand side is
\sim\#\mathscr{P}_{2g+1}\cdot Q_{r}(2g+1)
as
g\to\infty.
One checks the compatibility between this term and the main term

Q_{1,r}
in (
4
). This conjecture has been established only for
r\leq 3
(with explicit power-saving error terms) in
[
HR92
]
for
r=1,
and
[
Flo17c
,
Flo17b
]
for
r\leq 3;
in
[
Flo17a
]
, Florea also obtained a weaker version of the asymptotic formula for the fourth moment. The only results towards (
4
) are the asymptotics established in
[
Dia19
]
, for the third moment, and in
[
DPP21
]
, for a weighted fourth moment.

12.1.5
.

The connection between the moment-sums of quadratic
L
-functions and the traces
\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)
is given by
11.3.7
. See also
[
DP18
, Theorem 6.4]
. To obtain information about the traces
\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g),
we shall apply to Equation (
4
) the Weyl integration formula, which, for convenience, we now recall, see
[
Bum04
]
:

12.1.6 Theorem

(Weyl integration formula)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a compact connected Lie group and
\mathbf{T}
a maximal torus.
Let
du
and
dt
be Haar measures on these groups, normalized so that their volumes are
1.
Then, for any
\mathbf{C}
-valued continuous central function
f
on
\mathbf{G},
we have

\int_{\mathbf{G}}f(u)\,du=\frac{1}{\#W}\!\int_{\mathbf{T}}f(t)|\delta(t)|^{2}\,dt

where
W=N(\mathbf{T})/\penalty\mathbf{T}
is the Weyl group of
\mathbf{G},
and

\delta(t)=\prod_{\alpha>0}\left(e^{\alpha(t)/\penalty 2}-e^{-\alpha(t)/\penalty 2}\right)

the product being over the positive roots of
\mathbf{G}.

12.1.7
.

We apply Weyl’s formula when

\mathbf{G}=\mathrm{USp}(2r),\mathbf{T}=\left\{\operatorname{diag}\left(e^{\pm\sqrt{-1}\theta_{1}},\ldots,e^{\pm\sqrt{-1}\theta_{r}}\right):\theta_{j}\in\mathbf{R}\right\},

and

f(u)=\sum_{\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})}\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)s_{\langle\lambda^{{\dagger}}\rangle}(u)

where

s_{\langle\lambda^{{\dagger}}\rangle}

is the character of the irreducible representation of
\mathrm{USp}(2r)
with highest weight
\lambda^{{\dagger}}.
Since each
Q_{n,r}
in
(
4
) is a function of
q^{\pm s_{i}},

i=1,\ldots,r,
it is convenient to substitute

q^{-s_{i}}\!=q^{-1/\penalty 2}t_{i},

hence
|t_{i}|=1
for all
i;
we denote the resulting functions multiplied by the normalizing factor
q^{-2g-1}

by
\mathcal{Q}_{n}(t;g,q).
For
n\geq 1,

we define

\mathcal{T}_{n}(\lambda^{{\dagger}})=\frac{1}{\#W}\!\int_{\mathbf{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{n}(t;g,q)\psi(t)^{-g}\overline{s_{\langle\lambda^{{\dagger}}\rangle}(t)}\,|\delta(t)|^{2}\,dt

where
\psi:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{C}^{\times}
is the character defined by

\psi(t)=t_{1}\cdots\,t_{r},
and
t=\operatorname{diag}(t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm 1}).

Then Proposition
11.3.7
and the conjectural asymptotic formula
(
4
), combined with the orthonormality of the characters

s_{\langle\lambda^{{\dagger}}\rangle},

lead to the following:

12.1.8 Conjecture
.

For every
\lambda\subseteq(r^{g})
we have

\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)=\sum_{n\leq N}\mathcal{T}_{n}(\lambda^{{\dagger}})\,+\,O_{\Theta,\,q,\,r}\left(q^{(2g+1)(\Theta-1)/\penalty 2}\right)\!.

12.1.9
.

While the asymptotic formula (
4
) has a regular behavior in the sense that

Q_{n,r}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime};2g+1,q)

dominates the term

Q_{n+1,r}(\mathrm{s}^{\prime};2g+1,q)

for all
n\geq 1,
as either
g
or
q
gets large, the asymptotics of

\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)
behave quite differently depending upon various regimes of the
ratio
|\lambda|/\penalty g.
In what follows, we begin to investigate the asymptotic behavior of

\operatorname{tr}_{\lambda}(g)
by analyzing closely the first two terms

\mathcal{T}_{1}(\lambda^{{\dagger}})

and

\mathcal{T}_{2}(\lambda^{{\dagger}})

in the above conjecture. Our analysis of the first term, in particular, also suggests that the family
\{\mathcal{H}_{g}^{1,0}\}
satisfies homological stability for the twisted coefficients

\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}

in a range independent of
\lambda,
as in
11.3.18
.

12.2.
The first term

12.2.1
.

To study

\mathcal{T}_{1}(\lambda^{{\dagger}}),

we first note that the set

\Phi_{1}
consists of the roots of the form

\alpha=\sum_{i\leq r}k_{i}\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{r+1},

with
k_{i}=0
or
1
for all
i=1,\ldots,r.
From
[
DT23
, Section 7]
, one finds easily that

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(t;g,q)\psi(t)^{-g}=(1-q^{-1})\sum_{\epsilon}\frac{R_{1}(q^{-1/\penalty 2}t_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,q^{-1/\penalty 2}t_{r}^{\epsilon_{r}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}g}\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}(1-t_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}t_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}})}

the sum being over all tuples

\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{r})\in\{-1,1\}^{r}.

Here
R_{1}(\mathrm{t})
is given by

R_{1}(\mathrm{t})=\prod_{n\geq 1}B(\mathrm{t}^{n},q^{n})^{i_{n}\!(q)}

where

B(\mathrm{t},q)\;=\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}(1-t_{i}t_{j})\,\cdot\,\bigg(1+\frac{-\,2+\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-t_{i})^{-1}+\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1+t_{i})^{-1}}{2(1+q^{-1})}\bigg).

12.2.2
.

The function
R_{1}
is closely related to the generating function of stable traces. Indeed,

\displaystyle(1-q^{-1})R_{1}(q^{-1/\penalty 2}t_{1},\ldots,q^{-1/\penalty 2}t_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Exp}\Big(\,q\,\mathbf{Log}\Big(q^{-1}+\sum_{k\geq 0}q^{-k}h_{2k}(\mathrm{t})\Big)-h_{2}(\mathrm{t})-1\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda}T_{\lambda}\,s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}).

The first equality follows by comparing
R_{1}
with the expression obtained by setting
z=1/q
in
7.0.14
, and noting that
\mathbf{Exp}(-h_{2}(\mathrm{t}))=\prod_{i\leq j}(1-t_{i}t_{j})
, cf. §
2.6.8
. The second equality is
4.4.12
. Combining this last expression with the following lemma, we also see that
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
agrees with the function defined in the previous section.

12.2.3 Lemma
.

For any partition
\lambda
with
\lambda_{1}\leq r
, any
g
, and indeterminates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
one has

s_{\langle\lambda^{{\dagger}}(g)\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})=\sum_{\epsilon}\frac{s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(t^{\epsilon_{1}}_{1},\ldots,t_{r}^{\epsilon_{r}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{r}t_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}g}\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}(1-t_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}t_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}})},

the sum being over all tuples

\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{r})\in\{-1,1\}^{r}.

Proof.

Recall that

s_{\langle\lambda^{\dagger}(g)\rangle}(t_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,t_{r}^{\pm})=\frac{\det\left(t_{i}^{g-\lambda_{r+1-j}^{\prime}+r+1-j}-t_{i}^{-(g-\lambda_{r+1-j}^{\prime}+r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}}{\mathrm{det}\!\left(t_{i}^{r+1-j}-t_{i}^{-(r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}}.

Write the determinant in the numerator as

\det\left(t_{i}^{g-\lambda_{r+1-j}^{\prime}+r+1-j}-t_{i}^{-(g-\lambda_{r+1-j}^{\prime}+r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}=\;\sum_{\epsilon}\,\prod_{k=1}^{r}\epsilon_{k}\det\left(t_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}(g-\lambda_{r+1-j}^{\prime}+r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}

and note that the denominator can be expressed as

\det\left(t_{i}^{r+1-j}-t_{i}^{-(r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}=(-1)^{r(r+1)/\penalty 2}(t_{1}\cdots\,t_{r})^{-r}\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}(1-t_{i}t_{j})\prod_{1\leq k<l\leq r}(t_{k}-t_{l}).

In the numerator, replace

t_{i}

by

1/\penalty t_{i}

for all
i,
and note that

\det\left(t_{i}^{r+1-j}-t_{i}^{-(r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\epsilon_{k}\det\left(t_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}(r+1-j)}-t_{i}^{-\epsilon_{i}(r+1-j)}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq r}.\qed

12.2.4 Lemma
.

For sufficiently small

\varepsilon>0,
the function

R_{1}(\mathrm{t})
is holomorphic on the polydisc

|t_{i}|\leq q^{-\frac{1}{4}-\varepsilon},

for
i=1,\ldots,r.
Moreover, on this polydisc, we have the upper bound

|R_{1}(\mathrm{t})|<\big(1-q^{-4\varepsilon}\big)^{-2^{1+r(r+1)/\penalty 2}}.

Proof.

Using the simple equality

1+\frac{x-2}{2(1+q^{-1})}=\frac{x}{2}+\frac{2-x}{2(q+1)}

with

x=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-t_{i})^{-1}+\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1+t_{i})^{-1},

we can write

B=B_{1}+B_{2},

where

B_{1}(\mathrm{t},q)=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-t_{i}t_{j})\,\cdot\,\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1+t_{i})\,+\,\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-t_{i})\bigg)

and
B_{2}(\mathrm{t},q)
is given by

\frac{2\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}(1-t_{i}t_{j})\,-\,\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-t_{i}t_{j})\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1+t_{i})\,-\,\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(1-t_{i}t_{j})\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-t_{i})}{2(q+1)}.

Note that
B_{2}
has no constant term and that all monomials of degree two in

B_{1}

cancel out, that is to say
B_{1}-1
has no monomial of degree smaller than four. Thus, if

|t_{i}|\leq q^{\,-\frac{1}{4}-\varepsilon}

for
i=1,\ldots,r,
we have the estimate

\begin{split}|B(\mathrm{t},q)-1|&\leq|B_{1}(\mathrm{t},q)-1|+|B_{2}(\mathrm{t},q)|\\
&\leq 2^{-1+r(r+1)/\penalty 2}q^{-1-4\varepsilon}\,+\,3\cdot 2^{-1+r(r+1)/\penalty 2}q^{-\frac{3}{2}-2\varepsilon}\\
&<2^{1+r(r+1)/\penalty 2}q^{-1-4\varepsilon}.\end{split}

Applying the inequality
i_{n}(q)\leq q^{n}/\penalty n,
which follows from the well-known formula

\sum_{d\mid n}di_{d}(q)=q^{n}

(
n\geq 1
), and the familiar estimate
\log(1+y)<y
for
y>0,
we have

\log|R_{1}(\mathrm{t})|<2^{1+r(r+1)/\penalty 2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n-n(1+4\varepsilon)}}{n}=2^{1+r(r+1)/\penalty 2}\log\big(1-q^{-4\varepsilon}\big)^{-1}.

This implies the absolute and uniform convergence of the product
R_{1}(\mathrm{t}).
The upper bound follows by exponentiating the last inequality.
∎

12.2.5 Remark
.

By multiplying successively

R_{1}(\mathrm{t})

by suitable products of zeta functions, or inverses of such, one can extend

R_{1}(\mathrm{t})

even further. For example, the product

\begin{split}\prod_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq r}\!\left(1-z_{i_{1}}^{2}z_{i_{2}}^{2}\right)^{-1}&\prod_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\leq r}\!\left(1-z_{i_{1}}^{2}z_{i_{2}}z_{i_{3}}\right)^{-1}\!\left(1-z_{i_{1}}z_{i_{2}}^{2}z_{i_{3}}\right)^{-1}\!\left(1-z_{i_{1}}z_{i_{2}}z_{i_{3}}^{2}\right)^{-1}\\
&\cdot\prod_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}<i_{4}\leq r}\!\left(1-z_{i_{1}}z_{i_{2}}z_{i_{3}}z_{i_{4}}\right)^{-2}\cdot\,B(\mathrm{t},q)\end{split}

is
1+O_{r}\left(q^{-1-6\varepsilon}\right)
for

|t_{i}|\leq q^{-\frac{1}{6}-\varepsilon},

from which it follows at once that

R_{1}(\mathrm{t})

is holomorphic on any such polydisc, with small positive
\varepsilon.

This observation could be used to improve the estimates in Corollary
12.2.13
and
12.2.16
below. However, to simplify the arguments, we shall only make use of
12.2.4
.

12.2.6 Lemma
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
be distinct non-zero complex numbers such that
a_{i}a_{j}\neq 1
for all
1\leq i,j\leq r.
Suppose
h
is a symmetric function of
r
variables, holomorphic on a domain containing

\left(a_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,a_{r}^{\epsilon_{r}}\right)

for all

\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{r})\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}.

Then

\sum_{\epsilon}\frac{h(a_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,a_{r}^{\epsilon_{r}})}{\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}(1-a_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}a_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}})}=\frac{(-1)^{r(r+1)/\penalty 2}}{r!}\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\sqrt{-1}\right)^{r}}\!\oint\cdots\oint h(\mathrm{z})\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(z_{j}-z_{i})^{2}(1-z_{i}z_{j})}{\prod_{i,j=1}^{r}(1-z_{i}a_{j})(1-z_{i}a_{j}^{-1})}\,\frac{d\mathrm{z}}{\mathrm{z}^{r}}

where each path of integration encloses the
a_{j}^{\pm 1},
but not

z_{j}\!=0.

Proof.

This is
[
DT23
, Lemma 7.1]
.
∎

12.2.7
.

We apply Lemma
12.2.6
to the function

R_{1}(q^{-1/\penalty 2}\mathrm{z})\prod_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}^{-g}

with
a_{i}=t_{i}.
By Lemma
